PRAISE FOR THE THIRD EDITION

“Ms. Ament has written an engaging and useful guide to both teaching and performing the art of Foley. The book thoroughly covers all aspects of the craft: its history and evolution, the minutiae of recording footsteps and props, prepping and mixing. It is an excellent guide for both teachers and people who want to learn this largely unknown part of the post-production process.”

Harry Cheney, Professor, Lawrence and Kristina Dodge College of Film and Media Arts, Chapman University

PRAISE FOR THE SECOND EDITION

“This book is entertaining, informative and an absolute must-read for everyone who wants to make a career in sound, as well as being a very handy lesson for anyone involved in production on whatever level—and a particularly valuable aid for ‘media’ course tutors.”

Wendy Laybourn, Network Nine News

“Thanks to this publication, sound is appraised as a subjective art form. So whether you’re a novice (i.e. ‘what even is Foley?’) or a whizz (‘pfft, why do I need to read about Jack?’), there truly is much to learn from this book. Seek The Foley Grail.”

Matt Howsam, Raindance.org

“Comprising a virtual master class on leading-edge Foley techniques, The Foley Grail covers just about everything a practitioner or observer of the Foley process would need to create convincing sounds for a variety of film and television … a must-have text for any post professional’s bookshelf.”

Mel Lambert, CineMontage

This book teaches you how to master classic and cutting-edge Foley techniques in order to create rich and convincing sound for any medium, be it film, television, radio, podcasts, animation, or games.

Award-winning Foley artist Vanessa Theme Ament demonstrates how Foley is designed, crafted, and edited for any project, down to the nuts and bolts of spotting, cueing, and performing sounds. Various renowned sound artists provide a treasure trove of indispensable shortcuts, hot tips, and other valuable tricks of the trade.

This updated third edition features the following:

  • New chapters dedicated to Foley in games, television, broadcasting, and animation, as well as what is new in sound for media education
  • A multitude of sound “recipes” that include proven Foley methods you can immediately use on your own projects
  • A diverse range of case studies from well-known films, shows, games, and animation
  • Interviews with current sound artists from around the world

By exploring the entire audio postproduction process, this book provides you with an excellent understanding of where Foley fits in the business of filmmaking and is a perfect guide for both newcomers and experienced sound designers wanting to learn more about this art.

Accompanying the book are online resources featuring video demonstrations of Foley artists at work, video tutorials of specific Foley techniques, lectures from the author, and more.

Vanessa Theme Ament is the former Edwin F. and Virginia B. Ball Endowed Chair in Telecommunications at Ball State University, Indiana. She hails from the film industry in Los Angeles, where she was a Foley artist, sound editor, and voice actor for film and television. She has worked on many notable films, including Predator, Die Hard, Noises Off, A Goofy Movie, Dolores Claiborne, Malice, RoboCop2, and Platoon, which won the Academy Award for Best Sound in 1986. She received her PhD from Georgia State University in Moving Images Studies.

Acknowledgments

In this third edition of The Foley Grail, I am humbled by the many professionals who have contributed their insights and experiences to this endeavor. As someone who now teaches and writes about film sound, I could not be useful in the classroom, nor be able to access the wisdom of so many wonderful film industry professionals, were they not generous with their time and graciously supportive of my mission to document film sound in general and Foley more specifically. There are some common misconceptions about those who work in film: that they are pretentious, entitled, or removed from the world most others inhabit. I have not found that to be true for most of my colleagues. They are hard-working people who realize how fortunate they are to work in an industry that makes art and to be able to raise families in a privileged environment. However, they also are like so many working professionals: they get up early, drive to work, make deadlines, coordinate with others, get critiqued by their supervisors, worry about their next paycheck, help their children with their homework, and are happy to watch a ball game in the evening or on the weekend.

I wish to thank, yet again, David Stone, who teaches sound design at Savannah College of Art and Design, who for 20 years was my husband and who is a great dad to our son, Nathaniel. His illustrations for The Foley Grail have added humor and a light-hearted playfulness that I treasure. Dave began as an animator and moved into film sound, where he was a valuable addition to both television and feature films. He won the Academy Award for Best Sound Effects for his supervision of Bram Stoker’s Dracula (1992), along with Tom McCarthy, Jr.

I want to express my appreciation for guidance from Dr. Rick Altman, film scholar extraordinaire and a motivating force in my desire to pursue the goal of a PhD after decades in the film industry. I want to also thank Dr. Ted Friedman, who is an inspirational thinker and tremendous teacher at Georgia State University in Atlanta. So, too, must I thank Dr. Jack Boozer, who taught screenwriting at Georgia State and, along with being my dissertation advisor, introduced me to the magic of adaptation in film. Additionally, I wish to thank Dr. Alisa Perren, who opened up the world of media industries and production studies, which allowed me to better understand where I “fit” in the scholarly world of film studies.

I want to express my gratitude to the faculty and leadership in the Telecommunications Department of Ball State University. My five years at Ball State as an endowed chair in such a supportive and collegial program allowed me to explore many opportunities. I could never have expected my five years at this university to have been as formative and influential in my pedagogical expertise, yet as we often discover in life, we are continually surprised by what is presented to us as we travel our journey.

I must thank my son, Nathaniel Ament-Stone, who is now a lawyer in New York, but I still think of him as my greatest ally and strongest critic, both in my writing and in my personal interactions. We learn a great deal from our children, and he is one of my most insightful teachers. I also wish to thank Dr. Richard Edwards, who wears several hats in my professional life. Richard is a brilliant film scholar, gifted pedagogist, and relentless champion of my work. It is rare to find a colleague and friend who can pull out the smallest detail in my writing and suggest improvements, yet support my vision, whatever it may be.

Finally, I want to thank Routledge and Taylor & Francis for continuing to support my work. A special thank you goes to Katherine Kadian and Alyssa Turner at Routledge, as they have been with me through every inch of the process to bring this third edition to fruition.

Preface to the Third Edition

With the release of the second edition of The Foley Grail in 2014, my perspective on the world of Foley had become more nuanced. I was completing a PhD in Moving Image Studies at Georgia State University and about to embark on a five-year appointment at Ball State University as the Edmund F. and Virginia B. Ball Endowed Chair of Telecommunications. The fact that I was writing a dissertation, which is an industrious and highly academic exercise, while completing the second edition of this book, a practical and more accessible aspect of writing, embodied the perfect illustration of how I have lived in two worlds since 2009: that of a film practitioner with the added incarnation as a film scholar. These two worlds can collide and be in competition with each other for my attention and my passion. However, over the past years, since writing the first edition of The Foley Grail, I have evolved as I have taught courses in film sound, video production, ethics in media, culture and media, film history, film aesthetics, and several immersive courses that allowed students to experience a more professional approach to directing and producing films.

Additionally, I have designed two Massive Open Online Courses (MOOCs) utilizing the Canvas learning management system: one for Turner Classic Movies, Mad About Musicals, and the other for the University of California, Riverside, The Art and Craft of Remote Teaching. I have interviewed faculty who teach a multiplicity of disciplines, directed the video series Hey, Scotty Bear! to empower students to learn more effectively during the remote learning era of Covid-19, and have written Divergent Tracks: How Three Film Communities Revolutionized Digital Film Sound (Bloomsbury, 2021) in addition to several chapters and articles about film sound and online learning.

While at Ball State University, I found myself hosting Cinesonika, a festival and conference for sound academics and students, and bringing film industry professionals David Stone, Steven Lee, Peter Damski, Gary Rydstrom, and John Semper to Ball State to impart wisdom and practical advice to both undergraduate and graduate students. Additionally, I gave five, yearly presentations on my research and teaching experiences, as well as attended many festivals and conferences for film-makers and film scholars. While teaching Mad About Musicals, I was fortunate to share time with Ben Mankiewicz on Turner Classic Movies and to host the film Silk Stockings at TCM’s yearly festival in 2018. Over the past seven years since the second edition was published and because of these various experiences while at Ball State University, I have altered my perspective on what is essential to know about Foley and have reconfigured this new edition to be more effective for use as a guide for a class semester.

Most of the interviews collected for this updated edition were conducted virtually during the Covid-19 pandemic. I was not able to visit movie lots or sound facilities or even meet with people on their Foley stages or editing rooms. Instead, the interviews were illuminating in a new way, as part of each discussion included how the individual was handling the pandemic and the resulting isolation. A few of these professionals were able to work at home or work on their Foley stages as “closed sets” with no visitors. This edition includes an expanded selection of international Foley artists, additional perspectives of more supervising sound editors, mixers, and American Foley artists from Chicago and Austin, film students turned professional Foley editors, a production sound mixer and boom operator, a film trailer editor-mixer, and an animation director. I have also included a “roundtable” discussion of a team of Foley experts who work at a facility in upstate New York to illustrate the collaborative practices of a comprehensive Foley company. It is my goal to further amplify the experience and appreciation of Foley and to include more insights into the aspect of film sound that is receiving increased visibility.

Foley is a fluid craft. It takes on many colors and textures depending on the past experience of the artist, the aesthetic tastes of the culture, and the demands and budgets for the specific projects. By including as many voices as is possible within the constraints of any written endeavor, I hope that the reader can better understand and embrace the people, art, and profession of this essential aspect of postproduction sound.

Introduction

DOI: 10.4324/9781003008439-1

Postproduction sound is an area of filmmaking that is attaining a broader audience as more films are made that feature stunning, imaginative, and creative sound design. Action films, fantasy films, and most assuredly the recent trend of superhero films require sound that captures the viewer and helps transcend the ordinary world. However, sound design also includes the more subtle and nuanced aspects of the aural narrative, which can progress the story and collaborate with the film score. So, too, can postproduction sound enhance the real world of the characters who live their daily lives and captivate us with story, relationship, and plot.

Foley, the performative aspect of postproduction sound, holds a special place in the soundscape. The footsteps, props, and cloth movement of the various characters in film become more grounded and “real” when we connect their embodied sounds with the visual presentation. The Foley artist performs the “sonic personality” of the various characters in the narrative. Just as the actor creates the inner life of the performance, the Foley artist creates or highlights the aural life of the character. How the character walks and handles various objects is a focus for the study of acting. How the character’s walk and handling of objects sound is the focus of the Foley artist in film, television, games, animation, and web series. The skill of Foley takes years to master, and those who make a living as Foley artists must be skilled at both synchronization and mimicry. They must walk the characters and handle their objects with sync that has the right energy and feel, and they must mimic the personality of the character.

The Foley artist must also understand how picture editing manipulates the timeline, how cinematography captures the scene, what the sound editor’s work entails, and how the rerecording mixers will combine the production sound, the sound effects, the Foley, the replaced voices, and the musical score. But primarily, the Foley artist must have a sensitivity to what the actor and director intended in a scene, so that the performed sound supports, and does not distract from, the performance of the actor.

This third edition of The Foley Grail expands what the previous two editions have introduced and amplified. In this edition, there are stories of Foley artists from various nations, in addition to Los Angeles, the San Francisco Bay Area, New York, Austin, and Chicago. There are similarities among all Foley artists in their goals and approaches to the craft, but the differences are what is more interesting. The reason for this is simple: Foley is not a monolithic craft, nor is it a codified system that all must and do abide. While some film communities have workflows that are connected, others have constructed their own unique approaches to the addition of footsteps, cloth, and props to the sound design.

Additionally, included in this edition are more voices of those who do not perform, edit, or mix Foley, but are media professionals who must incorporate Foley into their work or have experience with those who do. Also, there are insights from professionals who have adapted Foley to other uses, such as film trailers and radio dramas. Several former sound design students who now work in the industry share their journeys and how school and the professional world are similar and different.

This book is divided into four distinct parts. Some of this material has been updated from previous editions, and new material has been added. Thirty-two additional professionals have been interviewed for this addition, and previous contributors have been contacted for updates. The four sections are constructed to better enable a reader new to Foley to build comprehension about the craft, while those who are familiar with it might read with fresh eyes. There are 15 chapters in this edition to correlate with a traditional college semester and to assist in guiding the student toward platforming understanding and skills.

Part 1 begins with a chapter that presents the historical development and evolution of Foley in the United States. The second chapter gives an overview of Foley in other countries, including Canada, England, France, Germany, Austria, Russia, Serbia, Italy, Spain, Brazil, Chile, Turkey, India, Pakistan, and Taiwan. Following this historical and cultural contextualization is a chapter that discusses the aesthetics of Foley as it is perceived and performed.

Part 2 contains the protocols and conventions of Foley, with the first chapter outlining the roles of the various professionals involved with postproduction sound and their connection to Foley. The next chapter offers a detailed guide for spotting, cueing, and editing Foley using the industrial filmmaking method as a blueprint that can be adapted to other types of media. The third chapter in this section gives guidelines for and examples of various theories and methods for designing a Foley stage, and the fourth chapter discusses the role of the Foley and rerecording mixers, how they perceive the mission of Foley, and how they assist that mission.

Part 3 is the practical application section of the book for those who want guidance on the art and craft of the Foley artist. This section contains six chapters that amplify the skills, uses, and “recipes” for Foley. The first chapter details how footsteps are performed. Following the guidance on footsteps is a chapter about the manipulation of props. The third chapter gives insights and hints on how to master sync as a Foley artist. The fourth chapter discusses Foley for animation, games, radio plays, and film trailers. The fifth chapter presents some basic conventions for how to make specific kinds of Foley sounds, and the final chapter in this section illuminates how some strange and special requests for the Foley artist can be addressed.

Part 4 consists of two chapters. The first chapter is a “roundtable” of various experts discussing a variety of topics, including a group of Foley professionals who work together in one company and who address how they work as a team while retaining a sense of individual artistry. Former sound design students discuss how they adapted to working at a major studio, and a production mixer reveals how her introduction to film sound led her to start an organization to assist women in sound. Also included is a brief mention of films by and about women in sound and a museum that is dedicated to film sound. The final chapter is a selection of quotes from various professionals who offer advice and insights about Foley.

This edition of The Foley Grail has retained the “bones” of the first two editions, but the reconfigurations and additions are intended to lead the reader into the world of Foley from a different entry point. The reader can choose to focus on any particular section that is appealing or start at the beginning and take the journey from the evolution of Foley to aesthetics, to the various professionals involved with postproduction sound, and to the nuts and bolts of Foley, culminating with the perspectives of professionals that will enhance the reader’s understanding of the work, life, and culture of the Foley artist.

PART | ONE

What Foley Artists Do and Why

The History, Theory, and Methods of Foley

Chapter | one

Holy Foley

The Evolution of a Craft

DOI: 10.4324/9781003008439-3

As I began my research into the beginnings and development of Foley, several things became apparent. First, there have been many myths and inaccuracies floating around that contribute to the mystery and misunderstanding of the origins and development of Foley. Second, the practitioners of the craft are very busy doing their jobs and have precious little time to document the history of how their craft came to be or how they approach the work itself. And, finally, those who study and report upon the craft and origins of Foley, or any aspect of film sound, and who are not from the culture view this world through a different lens and, thus, have different observations and conclusions from those who have “lived it.” As one who has worked in the film industry and now embraces the mission of academic reportage, my purpose is to bring to light the origins, development, and practices of Foley, as best we know, on the basis of documented sources and personal interviews with several key individuals who were a part of the development of the craft and the codification of the “system.” While I can only present the account of a “reflective researcher,” what I offer is a basic overview of the film industry when sound was new and later evolving, in addition to including a fair representation of the individuals who have contributed to the development of the craft of Foley, as well as those who add valuable insights into its historical development.

THE UNITED STATES

Many of us from the film industry assumed that Jack Foley—the man for whom the craft is named—was a sound editor who began to perform footstep cues for his own reels and, thus, began a career of doing those of other editors who had neither the talent nor the patience to do them. This, so it was understood, led to Jack then performing some props that were better left to him than the cutting room. Somewhere along the line, the rumors circulated that Jack was from England and that he was around in the 1950s and 1960s. It was said that he was from Universal Studios, and it was assumed that the editors and Foley artists who had worked at Universal must have known the venerable Mr. Foley. Almost everyone in postproduction sound had some kind of conception about Jack that we now know is inaccurate. I recall my appearance on Late Night with David Letterman in July 1989, where I continued this myth on national television. I now feel obligated to correct the record with every audience or class who sees the episode clip. So, let us begin with who Jack Foley was and why he is considered the pioneer of today’s craft of Foley.

Jack Be Nimble: Jack of All Trades

Jack Foley was a New Yorker of Irish descent, who grew up on Long Island. His family was Catholic, as was true of many Irish immigrants. Beatrice Rehm, a long-distance championship swimmer, worked at the phone company with Jack, who also played semi-professional baseball. They met through a mutual friend. She was Protestant, and this was a source of conflict in both families. They were secretly married and moved to the West Coast, where Jack hoped for a baseball career in a milder climate. Shortly after, they had a traditional wedding in New York, which united both families according to Catherine Clark, Jack’s granddaughter. Bea later converted to Catholicism.

In 1914, Jack and Bea moved to California, first to Santa Monica and later to a small town called Bishop, located inland in Inyo County. During WWI, Jack served with the American Defense Society and was charged with secretly guarding the local water supply from sabotage. Jack also worked at the local hardware store and raised his four children in Bishop. He was a regular part of the local community theatre as an actor, writer, and director, and he also wrote articles in the local newspaper. He was a cartoonist, so he found himself creating cartoon strips for the local paper as well. Jack, by all accounts, was quite the charming extrovert. His granddaughter would continually describe him as “a people person.” It appears that Jack was a man of many talents, so it should come as no surprise that when the farmers of the area sold their farms to Los Angeles for water rights and the town of Bishop needed revenue, Jack would be one of the people looking for some inventive way of sustaining his adopted hometown.

Jack Foley saw the potential for Bishop as a location for westerns in this new “moving picture business” from nearby Los Angeles. It was an exciting time for a person who was entrepreneurial. The film industry was new and being reinvented on a daily basis. Once Jack convinced the local storekeepers what an economic boon location shooting could be for Bishop, he set out to convince the small film studios in the Los Angeles area to come to his lovely town. It is important to note that the “picture business” was not developed in any sort of organized process. Much as the internet—with its social networking, its access to information, and its convergence of blogs, chat rooms, and online gaming—began in a chaotic and exciting way, so too was the environment for movies in the early part of the twentieth century. It was open season for anyone with “gumption” and ingenuity. Many film pioneers captured in biographies have these qualities. This is important because one must see that Jack Foley was not just a guy who wanted a steady job and income for his family. He was willing to take risks on a new and exciting business that was unpredictable and required flexibility and inventiveness from its participants.

By the time Jack was in his 30s, he had parlayed his budding connections to the film business into a career as a stuntman and double. He became an assistant to director William Kraft and eventually directed short subjects starring Benny Rubin. As was the convention in movies at the time, everyone pitched in where they could, depending on their talents and commitment. There were no established positions in the beginning. Job titles were not common practice, and people moved from job to job depending on the requisite skill set. Thus, when Universal needed to add sound to Showboat (1929), Jack was one of those who seized the opportunity to pitch in and help solve the latest problem brought on by this new technology—or opportunity, depending on one’s perspective.

When The Jazz Singer (1927) was released, Warner Brothers recorded small segments of sound for Al Jolson’s improvisational singing performance while leaving the rest of the film as a silent production. The method used was the Vitaphone sound on disc system, which was one of two technologies available. The second was the Fox-Case system, which utilized sound on film. Universal had just produced the silent film Showboat, which was based on the Edna Ferber book and not the Broadway musical. The film was not the famous Kern-Hammerstein musical, but with the use of this new sound technology music could be added, as well as some dialogue and synchronized sound effects. As David Yewdall relates in his book Practical Art of Motion Picture Sound, Universal rented the Fox-Case sound unit to record the music, voices, and sound effects for the film. Jack, among others from the various studios, took a class at the University of Southern California that introduced students to this new sound technology. When the studio was finally ready to put sound into the film, there was one sound stage, with the orchestra, singers, and sound people—including Jack—to put all the sound to the film in real time. Jack and some selected colleagues did hand clapping, footsteps, and any props they could muster, as well as background voices for the film. This was the beginning of a new path for Jack as a pioneer in this new field in filmmaking, which at the time had no name. Jack Foley had developed many skills in filmmaking, including director, actor, writer, cartoonist, baseball player, and stuntman, but it was his entry into the new sound technology that solidified Jack as the person to develop this new profession. It was invented at first out of necessity, expanded into an esteemed craft, and then later enhanced and per-fected by others to become a nuanced and respected field of sound effects with its own name, in honor of him.

“As the technology developed, he altered his performance techniques.”

As Jack Foley continued to perform sounds for films, focusing on props and footsteps with some cloth sounds, these sound practices developed into an integrated part of the filmmaking process. Jack worked on a regular sound stage with the same kind of sound support that would be afforded on the set with the cast in production. He had prop persons, recording experts, and sound assistants. As the technology developed, he altered his performance techniques. Sound was primitive during the formative years of Jack’s career as a soundman. There was only one track to record on, and this dictated the approach Jack would have to use. He had a piece of cloth he kept in his back pocket to be pulled out for clothing sounds, and he had a cane he used to create the illusion of more than one person walking at a time.

According to his granddaughter Catherine Clark, Jack would play with various gadgets in his kitchen to see how they sounded. Because no one had done this before, there were no debates regarding the art or the process. Jack developed his own techniques, which became standard for his assistants as well. Ms. Clark relates that what Jack did was a secret for quite a while, and even his family was not sure what he did at Universal in the beginning. When they visited him on the lot, he was typically in a suit and would sit on a bench working on his column for a showbiz trade paper or be in an office. His family never saw him work. It was not until closer to his retirement that his true profession became ubiquitously known in the industry.

As other studios acquired people who had worked with Jack, they developed their own systems for putting sounds to pictures. While Jack Foley was himself never an editor, other sound editors began to adopt his practices and developed training protocols for those who wished to be a “Foley walker.” His influence on the practice of performed sound effects solidified Jack as a legend in sound, and he was inducted into the Editors Guild as an honorary member and received a Lifetime Achievement Award from the Motion Picture Sound Editors.

The history of Foley as it is now practiced in film and television had its early beginnings with Jack Foley, but there were others who contributed to its development. They came from different backgrounds and had different experiences in sound. The live performance of sound effects was used not only in film but also in radio and television. From radio plays to television news, sound effects were beginning to be performed, recorded, edited, and mixed into every aspect of media, as technology would permit.

Chicken or the Egg?

Robert L. Mott, who was a generation behind Jack Foley, was a sound-man in radio and television. He had gone to New York University (NYU) to study screenwriting after serving in World War II. NYU had a radio show, and Mott, through connections at the school, apprenticed and trained to be a soundman for the programs out of the university on the radio station WGYN. Mott relates many of his experiences in his books Sound Effects, Radio TV and Film and Radio Live! Television Live! Mott is no longer with us, but when I spoke with him, I found him to be a delightful gentleman who had retired on the central California coast and, during his career, had plenty of experience working on radio and television shows. Mott had been a drummer, which contributed to his career as a television writer and fostered his interest in the drama of sound.

“Mott had been a drummer, which fostered his interest in the drama of sound.”

According to Mott, vaudeville, which was influenced by English music hall, was an important training ground for any person who was interested in creating sound for radio. In vaudeville, the drummer was responsible for highlighting gags and dramatic effects with the drum set. The drummer would typically take his cues from the actors. The audience became used to certain drum effects being used for particular gag sounds. When radio began to incorporate sounds into radio plays, the soundmen were sometimes these same drummers who had worked in vaudeville. This progressed into the use of records, gadgets, and other props. As they had in vaudeville, the drummers, now soundmen, took their cues from the actors. As radio drama progressed, the actors and soundmen would play off each other, which, of course, resulted in more sound effects supporting the actors’ actions.

In his book Silent Film Sound, Rick Altman reminds us that music was used to mimic the sound that would support certain actions on the screen. The convention of having sound support the story had been well established long before it was more specifically produced with the progression of technology. While vaudeville was utilizing the drum kit for sound emphasis, silent films were using an organ, live orchestra, or records.

What is important to note for our purposes is that once sound for radio became popular, it affected both film and television. It is hard to know just how one medium influenced the other, once they were contemporaneous, but it is easy to see how radio sound, film sound, and later television sound influenced their respective techniques. At the time, Mott’s job in radio and TV was referred to simply as the “sound-man,” even when many of the people doing the job were women. There was no separation between the use of recorded effects and props or footsteps performed live. One person did all of these. Nowadays, if one were to see a live radio show performed, the soundperson might be referred to as a Foley artist. But that is historically inaccurate. Even Jack Foley was not considered a Foley artist; that terminology came later. Foley and Mott were soundmen.

The Studio System

John Post, who was a sound editor and Foley artist, worked at Universal shortly after Jack Foley retired. While he never worked with Jack, the conventions that developed through the studio system and the Editors Guild set up the codified postproduction sound effects training from which Post blossomed into a respected artist and tutor for many of the Foley artists who came after him. He is an essential “link” from the days of Jack Foley, who developed the process, to the days of exploding growth in film sound. Post retired from the film industry and passed away in 2010. However, his recollections of the industry are invaluable for us to understand how the life of the Foley artist changed. Post created the Foley for such films as Christine (1983), Repo Man (1984), and Salvador (1986). Shortly before his death, Post was honored at a special event about sound in horror films at the Academy of Motion Pictures Arts and Sciences in 2009.

“Post was great at ‘walking’.”

Post was great at “walking” the footsteps of characters and was, in short order, asked to “walk” for other editors who were less skilled. This practice progressed into Post regularly performing prop effects because he could do them quickly and creatively, so he became a “Foley walker” and stopped editing. As Post related it, at the same time there were already people, mostly women, who had been dancers in movies, performing footsteps and some props for sound editors. This was a natural transition from the days of musicals where the dancing sounds were put in after the picture was shot, on a sound stage with a dancer who specialized in this skill. Most of these people were women, as the bias was that the women were more coordinated.

At one point, John Post was paired with another sound editor turned Foley walker, Duane Hensel. They did the Foley for all of the Universal television shows throughout the 1970s. As a team, they both had the perspective of having edited sound and knew what would be edited and what would need to be “Foleyed.” This set them apart from some of the other walkers who had never edited sound. In today’s “Foley-heavy” world of film, television, and streaming episodic entertainment, most Foley artists specialize in the performance of Foley and have not become sound editors.

“Today the formal promotion of training and responsibility has been all but abandoned.”

What Post remembered about the training system to become a Foley walker at Universal was an eight-year requirement as an assistant in sound editing prior to actually editing. The new editor would be assigned easy editing tasks. Only experienced editors were permitted to perform Foley for themselves or other editors. During that time in film sound, there were strict requirements for union membership and training. One would apprentice, then assist, then edit, and finally supervise. These requirements have been relaxed considerably as the years have progressed. Today the formal promotion of training and responsibility has been all but abandoned to conform to the rapidly changing requirements of modern technology.

When I was a young dancer in training, I performed in musical productions in San Diego. One of my dance teachers, who also directed some of the shows, was a former Our Gang actor, Bonnie Jean Gore. “Coach” Gore told me stories about how she replaced Ann Miller’s dancing sounds in postproduction, uncredited. At the time, I was unaware of this practice, but once I began replacing Foley dance steps for feature films, I gained a greater appreciation of what this skill entailed. MGM choreographer Hermes Pan reputedly performed dance Foley for Fred Astaire and Eleanor Powell. What is uncontested, and totally in character, is that Gene Kelly did his own dance Foley. Mr. Kelly was a perfectionist who insisted on doing his own stunt work and performed the recorded sounds for his dancing as well. Dancing is not always live action, and not always human. In the animated film Cats Don’t Dance (1997), the animals danced and the sounds were performed on the Foley stage. It was the last film project for Mr. Kelly. He was the consultant for the animated dance steps.

In the 1960s and 1970s, sound editors were in the union, but the newly codified job of Foley walker was not in any union. This remained true until the summer of 2006, when “Foley artist” was finally included as an official category of the Motion Picture Editors Guild. Until that time, any Foley artists that were in the Editors Guild were accepted in the category of sound editor. Some earlier Foley artists who had been dancers were in the Screen Actors Guild.

During Jack Foley’s time at Universal, there were changes in the venue where the sound would be recorded. Most of the time, Jack would be on a sound stage. An edifice was built for him to isolate his sounds. This would be known as “Foley’s room” or “Foley’s stage.” Now, the studio where Foley is performed is officially a Foley stage—an obvious homage. Desilu Studios, which had originally been RKO, built the first official record ing studio for Foley, which, according to Hollywood oral tradition, is when the term became official.

“The ‘longest-lived Foley team’ in the film industry.”

Ken Dufva and David Lee Fein for many years were the “longest-lived Foley team” in the film industry, having worked together as Foley artists for 21 years until the late 1990s. They worked first at MGM and later at Paramount. They both entered the system at a time when studios had a standardized progression into virtually all jobs in filmmaking, and the unions and guilds had a strong influence on the training and job protection of employees. Thus, Dufva and Fein have, individually and together, quite interesting perspectives on the growth of and changes in the world of Foley from the 1960s to the present day.

Dufva was assigned the job of projectionist at MGM in 1965. He was part of the team at MGM for 11 years and, during this tenure, moved to the Foley stage as the regular projectionist. As was common practice in those days, there was a great deal of job security and protection through the unions, and Dufva was very secure at MGM. However, it was tricky to move from a position in one union to a position in another union. When the opportunity came for Dufva to move to sound as an editor and Foley walker, it took some time for the position to materialize. Dufva was making the move from Foley projectionist to replace the retiring Jack Morissette.

David Fein’s entrance into Foley actually started at Gomillion Sound. He had worked on small independent films as an editor and moved into Foley as a natural next step. When he got the opportunity to audition to replace Scott Perry at MGM, Fein was very nervous. Although he had done some Foley at Gomillion, MGM presented a huge opportunity, and he did not want to make a bad showing. Fein remembers “feeling the eyes of some persnickety person” on his back during the audition. The audition resulted in Fein’s hire as a sound editor who walked Foley, and within months, when Dufva joined the team, their partnership began. For the first two years, Dufva and Fein worked with a third veteran, Lovell Norman, until his retirement. Norman was then replaced by another Foley artist, who was subsequently replaced when he retired. Eventually, it was just the two of them—Dufva and Fein. Even as the rest of Hollywood adopted the standard two-person Foley artist convention, Dufva and Fein were the last to lose their third Foley artist. Jack Foley may have been the first person in Hollywood to create the role of the Foley artist, but MGM had expanded the role to be far more substantial.

“Kenny and Dave,” as those in the industry know them, are both gentle in demeanor, respectful of each other, and professional in their work ethic. They had a high regard for each other and never had any conflicts arise between them. They got so used to working together that they did not even have to discuss who would do which cue or how they would approach it. They had a truly functional Foley “marriage,” which was admired by all who knew of them or worked with them. They attribute this to a lack of competitiveness between them, and one can quickly see when the two are together that they genuinely like and admire each other. However, they also were considered one of the best teams in Foley, and their work was highly valued. They handled an abundance of major films, including Robocop (1987), Ghost (1990), and The Godfather: Part III (1990), although until the late 1980s, screen credit was not regularly awarded to Foley artists. So, unless one does careful research or is from postproduction sound, they might never be aware of the number of films the team Foleyed. Dufva and Fein have also been eager contributors to any educational project regarding Foley, as they value passing on the traditions and informing others about the craft.

“Screen credit was not regularly awarded to Foley artists.”

Dufva and Fein started at a time when Foley was recorded on one track. Most of the prop effects were edited in, so their task was more focused on the footsteps of the characters. Having a team of three people as walkers allowed many characters to be covered at one time. Because recording and rerecording technology was still rather simple, dubbing mixes did not include any stereo mixing or panning, as is now the common practice. As long as principal character footstep cues were kept together and background footsteps were kept together, characters could be combined or “married” easily. This, of course, changed as technology allowed more separation of characters and props.

MGM had a nine-person team on the Foley stage when Dufva and Fein began working together. There were three walkers, a projection-ist, a boom person, a mixer, a recordist, and two prop persons. This tradition conformed to who might be on a sound stage for production. But as the years progressed and the system evolved, the tradition became two walkers, a mixer, a recordist, and a projectionist. The stage began to accumulate props, and there was rarely a need for someone to go gather props during a session. When Dufva and Fein were not performing the Foley, they were editing as they were both editors as well as Foley artists.

Ross Taylor and Kitty Malone were a Foley artist team for over 20 years. Taylor had been a sound editor who had edited in his own footsteps. Similar to John Post, Taylor had also been allocated as the editor to do Foley footsteps for other editors. Malone was a dancer on The Andy Williams Show. When the Tarzan television series—which was filmed in Mexico—needed Foley footsteps, someone who knew Malone suggested she be hired to replace the sound for the jungle hero. She continued on as the Foley walker (uncredited) for the series, even as she continued dancing for The Smothers Brothers Comedy Hour.

Once Taylor and Malone became a Foley team, they worked full time from 1971 until Taylor retired in 1992. They were a unique team as Taylor was a respected editor and Foley artist and Malone was an established dancer and admired Foley artist in her own right, as well as an actress. Malone continued to work until 2003. What distinguished this pair from Dufva and Fein as the longest lived Foley team is that they worked separately as well as together, and Malone also worked as an actress while Taylor also edited. While their partnership lasted as long, they were not a single entity—as Dufva and Fein had been—for the entire time.

Another recognized team in Foley was that of Margie O’Malley (Deneke) and Fred J. Brown [Conan the Barbarian (1982) and The Last Starfighter (1984)]. While O’Malley was strictly a Foley artist, Brown was a sound editor who performed Foley. As O’Malley began working in the San Francisco Bay Area with Jennifer Myers [The English Patient (1996) and Good Will Hunting (1997)] and Marnie Moore [Chocolat (2000) and The Singing Detective (2003)], Brown continued working in Los Angeles until 1997.

Realism

Robert Rutledge is included here as one from the transitioning Foley generation of John Post, although he was younger. His mother, Evelyn Rutledge, had been one of the first female sound editors, and as a young man Rutledge worked with his mother. While he inherited the profession, he established himself as a respected and gifted sound and dialogue editor and performed a lot of his own Foley [Wolfen (1981) and Fandango (1985)]. What was special about Rutledge, and the reason he must be included in the historical perspective, is that he understood the Foley stage as a place to recreate Foley to sound like production. Because Rutledge knew how to save production sound, he stressed the importance of having Foley do only what was required and have it sound like it occurred in production. Rutledge, along with his famous sound colleague Walter Murch, was known for his dislike of Foley that draws attention to itself.

“He had a special gift for ‘becoming the character’.”

Rutledge knew how to spot a show, pull the effects, supervise a team, walk and edit the Foley, shoot and edit the automated dialogue replacement (ADR), edit the effects, and supervise the final mix. He was a humble and unassuming man, who worked as an equal with other Foley artists rather than as their supervisor. He was a perfectionist and understood how Foley could achieve excellence in the performance of sound, while editing had its own tasks. Rutledge was one of the first to say, “You have to get the feel right. You can cut sync. You can’t cut ‘feel’.” Rutledge believed that the best Foley was intuitive, and he had a special gift for “becoming the character” in his Foley performance.

Rutledge trained his two stepsons, Scott and Gary Hecker, in these crafts. Scott is now an Oscar-nominated, supervising sound editor, and Gary is a veteran Foley artist who has expanded an inventive Foley practice.

Mr. Elegance

It would be impossible to leave this section of Hollywood Foley history without mentioning Charles L. Campbell, who has left an extraordinary body of work behind. Campbell began his career as an actor and worked in television and motion picture films as a sound editor and supervisor. However, his attention to Foley detail and his exercise in “shoe auditions” for characters in films was unique. Campbell supervised such films as Misery (1990), A Few Good Men (1992), and The River Wild (1994).

Campbell directed Foley artists with a meticulous methodology that translated to many sound creations. Campbell and his Foley artists operated as colleagues. Campbell passed away in 2013, but his contribution to Foley will continue to be felt for decades to come. He encouraged Foley artists to use the Foley stage as a design shop, and, as a result, several of his regular Foley artists expanded their practice to include more special sound effects created on the Foley stage, rather than limiting themselves to the more typical practice of performing the sonic embodiment of the characters in the film.

Campbell will be remembered for his soft-spoken demeanor and his elegant Henry Fonda–like walk. However, it was his determination that the Foley stage be used to design effects and his belief in the importance of highlighting the narrative of the film with sound that will be most remembered.

New York, New York

Elisha Birnbaum was a production mixer and sound editor in Israel. In 1955, he began to add what he later understood was Foley to his soundtracks. Birnbaum was the first Foley artist in Israel. In 1970, he immigrated to New York and started a sound company called Image Sound Studio. He had one Nagra recorder and a Scully two-track, quarter-inch, reel-to-reel tape recorder. His company was holed up in two rooms in the legendary Brill Building, and he had brought an abundance of quarter-inch sound effects tapes with him. He hired a young man by the name of Tom Fleischman—as a favor to the young man’s mother, picture editor Dede Allen—to categorize the effects and organize them as a sound effects library. Birnbaum used one of the two rooms to do minimal recording, including Foley.

Shortly afterward, Birnbaum was approached by the three owners of a small company, Sound One, to become their fourth partner. At that time, Birnbaum was the only Foley artist in New York and was essentially defining the New York style of Foley—more naturalistic and more like what he had heard as a production mixer. Birnbaum supervised and performed Foley for such films as The Untouchables (1987), My Blue Heaven (1990), and Shadows and Fog (1991). Sound One became the primary postproduction sound facility in New York and remained so for over 30 years. Birnbaum worked until 1996. As part of his contribution to the art of Foley, he trained several Foley artists, including Marco Costanzo, who is now located at c5 in New Jersey. Costanzo has maintained the high standards Birnbaum set. Birnbaum has said of Costanzo, “I am very proud of Marco. He was the most talented of all of my Foley students.” Birnbaum was a gifted Foley artist. He created Foley for many films, most of them uncredited. Additionally, Birnbaum provided employment for every sound professional to work in New York from the 1970s until he retired. Sound One had virtually no competition until c5 opened its doors in 1989 under the direction of supervising sound editor Skip Lievsay and Foley artist Ron Bochar. Tom Fleischman, who had built the first sound library for Birnbaum back in 1970, stayed at Sound One—having earned both Academy Awards and Emmys as a rerecording mixer.

Both Sides of the Bay

In the San Francisco Bay Area, there have been two primary postproduction sound facilities that have been recognized for their award-winning soundtracks. Skywalker Sound, nestled in Nicasio, Marin County, is a bucolic sanctuary owned by George Lucas, and the Saul Zaentz Film Center, which was located in Berkeley until 2018, was a respected film sound facility and home of Fantasy Records. Both Skywalker and Zaentz have garnered Academy Awards for sound, and both have distinctive approaches to Foley. Skywalker Sound employed Dennie Thorpe [The Right Stuff (1983) and Saving Private Ryan (1998)] as the lead Foley artist for over 25 years and has progressed to employ several other artists, including Jana Vance and Ronni Brown. Thorpe, who was primarily self-taught, had developed her own style of Foley workflow, utilized headphones as she worked—unusual for Foley artists—and trained Vance herself. Thorpe and Vance had not worked anywhere else, and as Thorpe retired, she passed the lead artist mantle to Vance. Brown had worked with Margie O’Malley, and after an established Bay Area career, she began working at Skywalker Sound in 2008. Presently, Skywalker boasts two Foley teams, with Vance and Brown performing most often as a team together. More recently, John Roesch and Shelley Roden have joined Skywalker Sound as part of the Disney expansion.

The Foley artists who worked at Saul Zaentz Film Center had also worked in Hollywood at times, at Skywalker with Thorpe upon occasion, and within the San Francisco film scene at other facilities as well. While Skywalker is home to a stable of sound professionals, those at Zaentz were often just as likely to work in Hollywood in addition to the Berkeley facility.

Both Murch and Splet were less likely to rely on Foley than other sound designers. They preferred Foley that was understated and would even go out and record themselves doing Foley in natural settings rather than record on a stage. Rerecording mixer Mark Berger [Amadeus (1984) and Mortal Thoughts (1991)] related a story about Foley on The Godfather: Part II (1974) that illustrates Murch’s iconoclastic approach to Foley. To capture the sound of the footsteps of someone walking up marble stairs, Murch and Berger had calibrated a metronome at frames per footstep using the flash feature, rather than the tick sound. Murch, Berger, and Pat Jackson then went to a building in San Francisco and put a microphone on the second floor. They started walking up the stairs in sync with the flash. When they got back to the editing room, they found they had walked the scene pretty close to sync. For Murch, this approach to Foleying footsteps, with the unpredictability of a natural setting, was preferable to the sanitizing that might occur on a Foley stage.

Chapter | two

The Progression of an Art

International Foley Practices

DOI: 10.4324/9781003008439-4

LES BRUITEURS: FRANCE

Messieurs Pinvin and Plasman were “Bruiteurs,” or “noise makers,” who worked together to create sounds for silent films in France. Pinvin performed the voices for the films, and Plasman created the sounds for the props. Plasman had been burned—his face terribly disfigured—in World War I, so he preferred to work in the shadows. The men worked with one track for all of their sounds. They were experienced circus workers and knew how to use a microphone and work with 16-mm film. The new American films were coming to France, so they were kept busy putting sounds to the films so that French audiences could enjoy these new entertainments.

Everything had to be performed at once. They would perform the voices and sounds, then stop. Then perform, then stop. If a speaker made a mistake, the Bruiteurs would begin again from the top. This process continued until the technology developed to allow editing and multiple tracks for recording.

Julien Naudin is one of France’s veteran Foley artists. His father was one as was his grandfather, who learned from M. Plasman. The Bruiteur profession is one of family tradition. Naudin has trained his brother, his wife, and his daughter to work with him in Foley.

In the Bruiteur tradition, the work of Foley is perceived as more of an art form. Rather than being considered part of an industrial Ford-ist tradition—as in Hollywood—Foley is considered an important part of the narrative, and the artist works closely with the director. Naudin’s father worked directly with Jacques Tati to create the whimsy in the sonic world of Monsieur Hulot. Additionally, the French tradition of Foley relies on stages that have different “rooms” for each area of a home. Rather than recording most Foley in one studio, Naudin’s studio contains areas separated by curtains that have kitchens with running water and a full complement of appliances, car interiors with working steering wheels and dashboards, and furniture appointments and surfaces that match any predictable interior. The consistency of Naudin’s Foley relies on several European studios all with similar configurations of working areas and props.

ACROSS THE POND: THE UNITED KINGDOM

The United Kingdom has several Foley artists who work regularly in film, television, and documentaries. One of these artists is veteran Peter Burgis. His background is unusual for a filmmaker, and especially for a Foley artist. Burgis began working in Devon, England, as a professional assisting people with learning difficulties. As a former member of the Royal Air Force, Burgis was attracted to working with troubled youth who were “absolute versions of myself,” and while he was part of a “volunteer scheme,” he began to make public service videos for an editing company in Cornwall. This set of endeavors led to presenting programs on the BBC, where Burgis “fell in love with what went on behind the camera.” While working at a small studio as a runner, he noticed the Foley stage “on the left” and “[it] was like opening Aladdin’s Cave for me.”

Burgis does not consider himself technically minded and prefers the physical creativity of performing Foley. However, he did find himself mixing Foley at a small studio with Foley artists Pauline Benion and Jack Stew. What Burgis admired was “the choreography between the two of them.” Burgis occasionally stepped in to help the two artists with background footsteps. It was Benion who advised Burgis that, “you need to stop recording and you need to start being a Foley artist.” This was in 1993, and while Great Britain was in an economic recession, Benion introduced Burgis to people who would hire him at a time when “old school attitude was the forefront.”

Burgis and his good friend Andie Derrick, an actress, decided to work as a team, and Derrick “practiced for three days in my flat” to prepare for her first day as an artist with Burgis. As an openly gay man, Burgis found some prejudice in the more traditional world of postproduction sound when he began, and he found himself being confrontational in response. However, what Burgis learned as he became more experienced and secure in his craft was “the best way to educate a man was with kindness,” which allowed his career as well as his professionalism to advance him to the status of veteran as postproduction sound in London progressed technologically and aesthetically as well.

“Burgis considers himself an ‘alchemist’.”

Burgis considers himself an “alchemist” when it comes to creating Foley. He subscribes to the suspension of disbelief, and when it comes to discerning what sound components to put together for a Foley cue, “if it sounds right and is telling the story I want to make, I use it.” He does not concern himself with using a recognizable prop for a sound if “another object has a better dynamic to it.” He has several favorite props that he uses frequently: a Zippo lighter he bought when he was 19 while in the Royal Air Force; his grandmother’s purse—“it’s got the most lovely snap;” his son’s wallet from his teen years; some Hush Puppies shoes that he has used for Daniel Radcliffe in the Harry Potter films—“he has dyslexia and has a funny walk;” and some moccasins made by Clarks.

Meltem Baytok and Richard James Hinton are Foley artists who primarily work in Bristol. Baytok is originally from Turkey and learned Foley in the United Kingdom before she could speak English fluently. She relied on reading actors’ faces, emotions, and reactions to understand how to do Foley. “It’s like being deaf and reading their bodies like sign language,” says Baytok.

Hinton edits his Foley as well as performs it. His Foley mixers originally worked on a PC-based digital system called Pyramix—from Switzerland—but now they work on Pro Tools to conform with industry standards. Hinton often works without cue sheets and pays close attention to the microphone technique when he records. In a smaller room, he positions the microphone more closely, but when he works on a larger Foley stage, he likes more distance on the microphone.

ONCE UPON A TIME IN THE BOOT: ITALY

When one thinks about Italian sound, the name that probably comes to mind is Sergio Leone. His films featured sound effects, Foley, and replaced dialogue to emphasize the narrative. Leone and Ennio Morricone, his composer, utilized a technique for scoring Once Upon a Time in the West (1968) before shooting a frame of film. The actors performed the scenes with the prerecorded score playing on the set to enhance the style, pace, and drama of the film. Italy has four Foley artists who are considered the veterans of the craft: Aldo Ciorba, Luciano Anzellotta, Italo Cameraccanna, and Sergio Basili. Foley and special sound effects for Leone’s films were performed by Ciorba, Anzellotta, and Cameraccanna. Basili began his career a bit after the first three but is included in the list of the earliest Italian Foley artists. Ciorba’s son, Marco Ciorba, continues his father’s tradition and techniques

for performing Foley in today’s Italian cinema. Speaking to me through his translator, director Riccardo Brex, Ciorba explained how he inherited the profession of Foley. Prior to the elder Ciorba, one of the sound creators was Tonino Cocciuttuolo. In Italy, the tradition of what corresponds to Foley began in the early twentieth century. Cocciuttuolo was famous for his performed sound effects. So, too, was Aldo Ciorba—who studied under Cocciuttuolo—and now Marco Ciorba continues as a highly respected Italian Foley artist.

In Italy, the practice of the craft varied from both the French and the American in one very specific way: the artist would sometimes go to the set and watch the production as it was being shot. This allowed the artist to be familiar with the sets, characters, and style of the film. Additionally, the Foley artist had a closer relationship with the director than is enjoyed in the United States. There was, and still is, a sense of collaboration with the director on the larger films.

Another Foley artist in Rome is Mauro Eusepi, who began his career at Cine Citta where he discovered sound as an important component of filmmaking. After studying new media and the science of sound at University of Rome Tor Vergata, Eusepi began working as an intern in 2005 at Dreamsound, a postproduction sound facility, and in 2009 he committed to a career as a freelance Foley artist. Presently he has his own Foley stage on the east side of Rome in the Pigneto neighborhood. Eusepi agrees with Aldo that recording Foley outside is a preferred method for more naturalistic sound and for bigger props, as the stage itself is rather small and better suited for footsteps and small-prop work. Eusepi prefers sound that resembles production as much as possible: “[y]ou can tell the difference between production and artificial sound.” To that end, he will save as much of the production sound as possible, with the intention of having sound effects and Foley that “reproduce the color of the production sound.” He adds that the modern Italian director has altered the sound aesthetic from earlier Italian films by leaning toward using more production sound, as Italy’s signa ture of totally reconstructed sound tracks from beginning to end of the film has evolved over the last few decades.

“The Foley would be performed in a natural setting.”

At times, and perhaps due to the influence of Italian neorealism, the Foley would be performed in a natural setting—outside in a forest, as an example—to capture the right sound and ambience for the footsteps.

DAS LAND DER DICHTER UND DENKER: GERMANY

Germany has a well-developed film industry, so it is no surprise that the country can boast several established Foley artists, such as Martin Langenbach and Peter Deininger. One of the more recent additions to Germany’s Foley artist stable is Niklas Matthaei, who has his own studio after working only a few years as a Foley artist. Matthaei had studied literature, philosophy, and music, but he changed his educational trajectory to enroll at the Akademie Deutsche POP in Berlin to study sound. Afterward, Matthaei tried to find work at various sound design and mixing facilities. He discovered he was better at performing and mixing Foley, and by 2019, he was working full time as a Foley artist.

In Germany, it is not unusual for an artist to self-record. While some German sound professionals use Nuendo, Matthaei uses Pro Tools, which has become the recognized industry standard. The cueing for Foley is part of the mixer’s responsibility, and there are usually two spotting sessions with the director. Matthaei does not have a favorite genre of film to work on, but he prefers “something with meaning that is challenging.” Additionally, he loves to spend time getting a sound right because he prefers to not have to redo or alter it. “I am not really good at compromising.” While some believe a Foley artist should use the exact prop that appears on screen, and others might “do everything with that one key,” Matthaei considers himself “somewhere in between” and prefers to receive much clarity and information from the director “to get the drama right.” He enjoys doing the small and sensitive props that add nuance to a scene. When it comes to performing the footsteps, he believes he should “give as much as the actors on set” to get “emotion in the steps.”

NEIGHBORING AUSTRIA

Cornelia Baumgartner, who lives in Bregenz, Austria, near Switzerland and Germany, studied visual media literacy and is a composer and singer. These combined areas of expertise have led Baumgartner to a career in various areas of sound design. Beginning as a film composer, Baumgartner relates that she expanded her work into sound design, using sound libraries and creating “dark atmospheric” soundtracks. Her experience as a pianist led her to working with midis and digital audio workstations (DAW).

One of Baumgartner’s vocations has been to design sound effects and Foley for audio guides for cities. “I wanted to create cinema in the ear,” she remarks. In these audio guides, actors portray historical characters, and Baumgartner incorporates the voice acting in her projects. Bregenz is a small city with a population of about 30,000, and there are no Foley stages nearby, so Baumgartner performs her Foley in the quiet areas of her home.

“‘The search for the perfect sound’.”

Baumgartner finds projects in animation and commercials for advertising agencies, and she has recently designed sound for an episodic series. In addition to taking a sound design workshop in Bristol, England, she visited a Foley artist in Vienna on a small Foley stage. Through her studies and her professional experiences, she finds she is on “the search for the perfect sound” and enjoys “adding more levels of sound” to add a sense of hyperreality. One illustration of her approach to layering sound is the sound of a fishing reel used for the squeakiness of a mountain bike. Baumgartner summarizes, “in every object lives a sound.”

THE FORMER YUGOSLAVIA: SERBIA

Misa Jakovljevic, Serbia’s primary Foley artist, was introduced to what he calls “Foley magic” in 1995 when working as an assistant engineer in a postproduction facility. At the time, Serbia was in the midst of political and social upheaval and Jakovljevic was a young man. He made the “mistake” of walking onto the Foley stage and saw what the three Foley artists—who worked in the dark so as to keep their craft mysterious—were doing. From that day on, Jakovljevic waited patiently for his opportunity to engage with the three famous artists who had worked in the former Yugoslavia and had been the first Foley artists in his country.

Marko Rodic, Sinisa Jovanovic, and Vladimir Tomanovic were the three Foley artists on the stage that day. Jakovljevic was working in the capacity of what would have been a recordist in the United States at the time—keeping track of the magnetic film stock, coordinating the cues on the cue sheets, and doing the general maintenance of the administrative duties in the control room. His lucky break into Foley “magic” came one day when the artists were having difficulty creating an appropriate prop for a “walking tree.” Jakovljevic said, “I have [an] idea. I need light!” Rodic let him into the sacred space of the stage, and after Jakovljevic created the prop for the artists, they told him to get out! But he began training with the three of them.

Rodic was the owner of the studio—Markoni—and Jakovljevic later bought the studio and continues the Foley tradition in Serbia. The economy is difficult in Serbia, and, for this reason, he has been the only Foley artist in Serbia. No one has the time to train for free instead of getting a job that would pay immediately. Jakovljevic loves what he does and is a jokester. “All day I stay alone in [a] darkened room and think how to make what [is] in [the] director’s mind and I must be quiet. When I walk out of that room, I like talking!”

MUSING ABOUT MOSFILM: RUSSIA

During the Stalinist era in Soviet Russia, there was a renaissance in culture. According to Pavel Doreuli—who learned his sound design skills while working in the sound library at Mosfilm—the history of Russian sound design is uneven, but it has some interesting peaks. This Stalinist renaissance was “not from [the] previous Russia, but from [the] new Russia,” says Doreuli, “new artists, new composers, totally new music!” Mosfilm was infused with state money to develop a national film industry. While there were no specific terms that delineated sound designer or supervisor in Russia, there was the development of the sound engineer. This profession included anyone who could edit or design the sound effects for film. The 1970s also proved to be a period when Mosfilm first began to categorize the sound effects library in any sort of codified fashion. Two women were hired, and they stayed at Mosfilm until the 2000s putting voice slates on each sound effect and devising a library system for editors.

The once dynamic Mosfilm became stagnant after the fall of Communism. In the 1990s, Mosfilm tried to cultivate the “Russian blockbuster,” beginning with Nightwatch (1994). However, the American blockbuster style did not coincide with the Russian cultural sensibility. The “Russian blockbuster” was a failure, but the American blockbuster remained popular.

In 2001, director Karen Shakhnazarov began to revitalize Mos-film. He conglomerated several independent film companies together and began the task of digitizing the sound effects library. Doreuli was charged with the digitizing task. As part of his tenure at Mosfilm, Doreuli worked with various sound engineers and encountered the various Foley artists that worked there. These Foley artists have stayed at the forefront of Russian filmmaking from the 1970s until contemporary times. Recently, two of these women, Ala Meichick and Faina Yampolskaya, have begun training new, young artists. Doreuli recalls that the most famous Foley artists in Russia were Sergei Figner and his father, Edgar.

TRANSCONTINENTAL: TURKEY

When Meltem Baytok began her Foley career in Bristol, UK, there were no Foley artists in her home country of Turkey. She began her career working on nature documentaries, and as her career has expanded, she has contributed to British television shows and films. Although she attempted to bring Foley to Turkey in the earlier years of her work in film, she found the climate inhospitable to the endeavor.

Over the past decade, there has been an expansion of professional sound and Foley in Turkey. Aleyna Seymen, a 23-year-old Foley artist in Turkey, explains the lack of Foley in Turkey: “[n]obody in Turkey wants to be a Foley artist.” Seymen knows Baytok and agrees that the United Kingdom was the best place for her to begin her career. However, Seymen began her career as an assistant to other artists to learn Foley, while she researched Foley artists in other countries. Her ambition to excel in the nascent beginnings of the craft in Turkey propelled her to write to veteran British Foley artist Peter Burgis to ask for guidance. Burgis encouraged his colleague Paula Boram to travel to Istanbul to mentor Seymen. According to Seymen, there are two other Foley artists in Turkey, both of them male. As the only female artist, she experiences a sense of sexism in the field of sound and explains, “[t]hey always try to give me up” when working with male editors or mixers. After several frustrating attempts to be taken seriously and allowed to make creative decisions, Seymen made two key decisions to enhance her professional agency: (1) work with female Foley mixers and (2) work closely with her directors, who trust her taste and artistry. She prefers to work closely with her director to discern “what he wants me to feel” with the Foley. To that end, she enjoys having the director on the stage and perceives Foley as a performance that excels under a director’s expressed vision. Seymen has worked most often for the two sound companies Melodika and 1000 Volt Production. The budgets for Foley tend to be small and there are gaps between projects, but this is not unusual in a younger film sound community.

CONVIVIALITY: CANADA

Andy Malcolm is a Foley artist in Uxbridge, Canada. Prior to becoming a Foley artist, Malcolm was an assistant sound editor in Toronto and trained with two British sound professionals, Al Streeter and Peter Burgis, who had come to Canada from London to kick-start the feature film, postproduction, sound editing business.

Malcolm had studied mime, which turned out to be a helpful prerequisite for work as a Foley artist. Mime taught him how to break down the body into its basic elements and build them up in different configurations to mimic the body actions of the characters on screen. This helped Malcolm to better understand how to give weight and character to the footsteps of the actors.

Because Malcolm has a reputation as the premier independent Foley artist in Canada, he has designed his own workflow, including a different set of protocols for his team. He has mixers, Don White, Kevin Schultz, and Jack Heren, a separate Pro Tools operator, Foley editor Jenna Dalla Riva, and his Foley artist partners Goro Koyamo and Sandra Fox.

Malcolm returned the favor of having been trained to be the top Foley artist in Canada by training one of Ireland’s Foley artists, Caoimhe Doyle. Doyle had watched Malcolm work in Ireland. She visited Canada to apprentice and train with Malcolm at his facility. Doyle then went back to Ireland to design and build a dedicated Foley stage at Ardmore Sound, where she now works with Foley mixer Jean McGrath.

“‘The jazz musicians of sound effects’.”

Chris Szott, a Foley artist in Edmonton, Canada, began his training with Malcolm and Koyamo as an apprentice for six months. He “picked up Andy’s team’s acute attention to detail,” and after completing his work with Malcolm, he proceeded to establish his own solo career. As the sole proprietor of Little Hook Sound, Szott is presently the only artist in Alberta. He and his Foley mixer, Isael Huard, work as a team on features, local productions, documentaries, and some series for Netflix. Szott studied recording engineering and music composition in London, and he asserts that Foley artists are “the jazz musicians of sound effects,” adding “every sound needs an intention.”

For Szott, performing props is a more accessible skill than perfecting the footsteps for a character. He considers the textures and frequencies of the prop cues, and he compares Foley to “learning a new instrument … it’s a life-long pursuit.”

Huard, who studied music technologies at the University of Alberta, considers Foley a highly collaborative task between production and post-production, as well as between artist and mixer. He likes to know what mic was used in production and will record Foley with the same mic if possible. He records using a room mic, in addition to a mic aimed at the footsteps or props. Szott and Huard record the Foley with an ear toward including a premixed track for the final dub (mixing in the box). Because Alberta is a smaller market for films, the team typically works 5–7 days on a feature or 4 days on a Netflix episode. Huard explains, “I use the tools to achieve what I need.” Because the team works on its own, “we have gotten used to what we like and understanding how what we hear gels.”

Both Huard and Szott like “pure Foley,” which translates for them as “the correct sound without relying on plugins.” After almost three years of working together, the musicality and emotion of the Foley is a joint goal. Huard’s training in manipulating sounds with electroacoustic music “informs a lot about how I hear sounds.” Szott adds, “sometimes when you get into the groove, you need to go back to the story.”

BOLLYWOOD

As one of the most prolific film-producing nations, India has a long history of lavish, music-filled films. Karan Singh, a Foley artist in Mumbai, began his career at the respected BR Studios, where his father had been a security guard. In 1984, at just 12 years old, he learned the world of film sound and would “sneak into the studio” to watch the Foley artists, including Prahlad Salvi, work on their films. Foley artists recorded to one analog track at a time for Foley, which meant all sounds had to be performed at one time and in excellent sync. Singh estimates that there are about 15 Foley artists in India, and most of the Foley work is performed by about three of these Foley artists. While at BR Studios, ADR was recorded during the day and Foley was performed at night. Most artists worked 18-hour shifts, which is an unusual practice compared to other international film communities.

“‘Every shot is in my brain’.”

In 2009, after several years as a freelance Foley artist, Singh opened his own studio. Foley tends to be an extensive and elaborate aspect of Indian sound design, and he often works on four or five films a month. In addition to Bollywood films, Singh performs Foley for films produced in Bangladesh. Using the production track as a guide (he refers to it as the location track), he figures out the Foley cues during the session, on the stage: “every shot is in my brain,” he explains. Foley artists might work on four or five films in a month. “We have to come very close to the nature of sound,” says Singh.

Singh measures the amount of time he works on a film in hours, rather than days or weeks, and estimates that he might work 100 hours on a romantic comedy (rom-com) and close to 300 hours on an action film. He focuses on all of the details he can see in the scene: “when I see a film, any film, I can make out that this film, Foley is important, and these sounds are going to be done. That time, then and there, I decide, and I really give attention to the detail.” Singh does not differentiate between the amount of detail for feature films or television. He approaches both types of projects in the same way. “We are making realistic films now, and we have to be as authentic as possible.”

Singh explains that the workflow differs from that of some other countries. The sound designer will also mix the sound for the film, and the associate to the sound designer might mix the Foley. Singh’s Foley mixer, who he refers to as a Foley engineer, is most often Ram Nath. The team typically uses only one microphone for a Foley session, unless the sound designer asks for a second microphone to assist with perspective. Singh’s focus is on balanced tracks and admits, “cloth is the most difficult thing to do.” He performs one cloth track to cover any principal actors and then wears clothes that contribute a bit of cloth rustle for background footsteps. Singh’s commitment to a realistic sound is modified for “superhero” films, which he performs using more loud and stylized methods. “There are some sound designers who want it to be loud,” and he alters his more realistic aesthetic sense to accommodate them.
