Singh will often pick up props anywhere he sees an interesting piece. An example of this is a stool he discovered at one of his favorite eating spots. The stool had a great sound, and he bought it from the owner of the establishment.

PIONEERING: PAKISTAN

Foley is in its nascent stage in Karachi, Pakistan. Kashif Ejaz began his career in Foley in the last decade is also a sound effects editor. As a child, Kashif cannot remember when he was not interested in sound. While working at a TV channel in Karachi and taking courses at a film school, he visited mixing stages in London, Istanbul, Bangkok, and Dubai. He observed the workflows of the various sound professionals to adapt their practices to his own sound editorial business. In addition to editing sound and performing Foley, Ejaz records the ADR for his projects. “Sometimes the directors are there, sometimes not.”

Pakistan’s show Pepsi Battle of the Bands (2017) on Pakistan Television Corporation gave Ejaz an opportunity to work as the recording engineer and postsound supervisor on the series. Pakistan’s small film industry allows Ejaz opportunities to wear several hats (editor, mixer, artist, musician), and he worked on Zero Dark Thirty (2012) recording special sound effects. While Ejaz enjoys his work in postproduction sound, he says that, in Foley, “footsteps are more challenging. Putting them into the scene is difficult.” He considers the distance the footsteps are from the screen, with the goal of having the footsteps fit in with production sound. Utilizing only one studio mic, he will add reverb on another track if he feels it is warranted.

Ejaz does not utilize one track for cloth, which is the more common standard for most Foley artists. Instead, he separates out characters’ clothing on several tracks. Because many Pakistani films require a large amount of ADR, the cloth tracks add life for each character. He emphasizes cloth in his animation projects.

TAIWAN

Master Hu Ding Yi began working at Central Pictures Corporation in Taipei in 1975. Central Pictures was founded in 1953 and has produced films by some of Taiwan’s major directors, including Ang Lee and Edward Yang. Master Hu began as a studio assistant and learned production mixing and postproduction sound. His interest in Foley led him to watch and study carefully the artists at the studio, and by the early 1980s, he was working primarily as a Foley artist.

Master Hu, who communicated with me through translator Zi Xian Lin, told me that when he worked with analog sound four or five Foley artists might work at the same time to maximize the sounds in a cue; however, the transition to digital Foley mixing has altered this workflow. Now he normally works alone or with one assistant. He usually watches a cue one or two times before performing it. In the film Vive l’Amour (1994), he had to walk the main female character for 6–7 minutes in one take. He finds footsteps to be more natural for him than prop work: “It’s not hard to do sound, but you have to take it to your daily life, like unconsciously observing the sound around you,” says Master Hu.

“‘We have to really feel the character’.”

Hu’s perspective is that the quality of the character performed is very important: “we have to really feel the character … like an angry man putting down a coffee cup.” He recalls a strange prop cue for the film Who Killed Cock Robin (2017), when for a gruesome scene he had to do the sound “for digging out a character’s eyeballs, and that’s something that does not exist in the sound library, so I had to create it.” He adds that the sound “must be vivid, and I like to challenge myself to make different sounds using diverse props.”

While Hu has worked on many Kung Fu movies in the past, he finds the emphasis on realistic films is the trend now in Taiwan. He performs Foley for films from Hong Kong, but he remains in Taiwan and does not travel.

When asked how Foley is different for television shows in Taiwan, Master Hu answered, “[t]here is no Foley for TV shows.” Although he has not worked on any of the musicals made in Taiwan or Hong Kong, he would like to do so.

Master Hu shares his philosophy about being a Foley artist:

You have to have a steady and strong mind set. Do not panic. Sometimes you think you did great job but the [mixer] asked for one more take, you better answer positively “yes.” Do it again, if the [mixer] says it needs to be better. Otherwise, you can’t manipulate the actor’s emotions from the picture because you have [an] unstable mind.

Master Hu has some thoughts for those who want to become a Foley artist:

After digitalized of sound workflow, we separate movement in many short parts and finish them one by one. Which is more friendly to [a] new learner but in my opinion, that’s not going to make you a great Foley artist. You have to capture the whole scene and do them within a take. When you learned how to manipulate each sound for a scene, that’s the moment that you know you are qualified for this job.

LATIN AMERICA: CHILE AND BRAZIL

Latin America’s developing professional sound community has exploded recently and includes a plethora of Foley artists. One of these artists, Mauricio Casteñeda, lives in Chile and works in his home country as well as Brazil. Casteñeda’s father ran a radio station, which fueled his love for music, and after studying sound engineering, his interest in film sound led to him to work as a sound assistant during the late 1990s at Filmosonido in Santiago. When he watched the Foley artists on the Foley stage, “it was love at first sight,” says Casteñeda. Some of the earlier artists performed their Foley outside.

Daniele Fillios, an editor, said, “you are doing it wrong,” and some of them were sent to France to learn new techniques in late 1999 and early 2000. Roberto Espinoza, Casteñeda’s Foley teacher, was one of those who learned the French methods for Foley. Espinoza wanted to take on the mixing duties, and Casteñeda began his Foley career. He credits the mixers he has worked with for encouraging him and helping him improve his technique and art. In 2015, he left Filmosonido because home sound studios were hurting business; he remarks, “I had to start my own Foley studio.”

Casteñeda also works in Brazil, and as he notes, “Brazil did not have the passion for Foley,” so he travels from Chile to Brazil to perform his Foley. His enthusiasm for Foley and his desire to “be like another character in the movie” motivates him to “offer more … I try to add something … you have to have your moments.”

GLOBAL PERSPECTIVE

International Foley artists provide different perspectives and insights regarding performing, mixing, and editing Foley. Many international Foley artists utilize techniques and methods similar to those of the United States, while others have adapted their own methods for performing Foley.

Expansion of our understanding of the cultural differences that exist in the world of Foley can enhance film literacy and enhance appreciation for artists outside of the United States.

Chapter | three

Aesthetics of Foley

Noise, Sound, Single, Married

DOI: 10.4324/9781003008439-5

NOISE VS. SOUND: FOLEY AS IT IS USED IN THE VARIOUS GENRES

When film or television viewers choose to watch the latest major film release in a movie theatre or stream films, documentaries, or television and web episodes on a television or another electronic device, they are not wondering who did the Foley or what Foley will be in the soundtrack. This minor obsession is the territory of the sound expert or fan. However, the viewer will be affected, albeit unknowingly, by the sound cues meticulously designed, cued, performed, and edited. The sonic experience will either enhance or distract. Sound design has impact, and Foley is a critical component of the overall soundscape.

A notable number of films are lauded as having great sound. What denotes an exceptional sound job is up for debate, but sometimes even the most celebrated films are actually loaded with so many sounds that the ear is overwhelmed. An overtly loud soundtrack containing multiple noises in it is not necessarily the best, most artistic, or tasteful track. An expert in the professional sound community and the film critic might both agree that adding “low end” and reverb or complicating the intelligibility of dialogue for dramatic effect helps a track seem more powerful, emphatic, bold, or even hyperbolic. However, that does not necessarily translate to a track with ingenuity or color. It might simply be loud, obtrusive, and self-conscious. Some of the most inventive and memorable soundtracks are more sparing in sound elements, creating a story world that is unique, specific, and decidedly understated. One can recall such artistry in films such as Barton Fink (1991), Road to Perdition (2002), All is Lost (2013), and The Revenant (2015).

The same aesthetic concerns exist in Foley, and while this aspect of sound design is less noticeable than the more dramatic sound effects that help drive the narrative in a film, similar challenges can arise. There can be an emphasis on doing as many sounds as possible to fill the Foley tracks. However, at the final dub, mixers rarely complain that there is not enough Foley. The more frequent complaint is that there is too much Foley. There is often duplication with what has been covered properly in sound effects. One quality of the most experienced Foley artists is to be discriminating about what should be heard and what should be ignored. They learn to help focus the scene with the Foley, not clutter it. The ability to discern what is necessary and what is not becomes even more crucial as the technology allows more choices in shorter time spans.

“What do you want to hear?”

The ear can only discern a limited number of sounds at one time. It is a much better strategy to make specific decisions regarding what sounds should be heard rather than make noise for everything that moves. The issue is not what sounds are actually there, nor is it what should be heard or what would be heard. As veteran Foley artist Alyson Dee Moore points out, the best Foley artists ask themselves, “What do you want to hear?” Moore’s point is this: you are designing sound. Make the artistic decision. Many of the most renowned sound experts in film and television share this sentiment. The goal is not to duplicate or replace every sound as it is in the real world or as it was recorded in the production track. The goal is to perform the Foley to support the narrative, honor the style and vision of the director, and collaborate with edited sound effects to complement the overall sound design.

Many films demonstrate an effective use of Foley in film. Foley is a supporting player in these films and can be studied to better understand its intrinsic value to the narrative in the film. Foley is used for different purposes, depending on the genre, style, and intention of the director.

Sound as Storyteller

Although many excellent films that can illustrate the power of Foley in supporting and at times even driving the narrative are available, I find I still prefer to use one of my favorite examples from Once Upon a Time in the West (1968), the epic film by Sergio Leone. The opening credits scene, which is about 13 minutes long, is introduced using minimal dialogue. The sound and dialogue have been completely reproduced in postproduction. The edited sound effects and Foley tell the story. By watching the scene, one can discern a great deal about the characters, the introduction of our key antihero protagonist, and the importance of sound to drive the story and evoke emotion. Three men appear at a train station, slowly and with intention, and proceed to wait. They methodically enter the station, walking on a hollow wooden floor, their dustcoats flapping in the wind. The ticket attendant speaks a few words, which are replaced dialogue, and tries to sell them tickets to the train, which one of our characters tosses into the wind. One of these three men (Jack Elam) has a battle with a fly. He sits calmly in a creaking wooden rocking chair because it is so hot, and he barely moves while he tries to get the fly out of his face. He finally captures the fly as he smashes his pistol barrel against the rocking chair where the fly has landed. The sound of the fly on his face, on the bench, and in the pistol barrel highlight the character’s patience to wait, kill time, and do as little as possible. Another character (Woody Strode) has water dripping on his hat from a leak in the roof. Each drip is carefully and artfully designed to emphasize the slowness of the action in the scene. He notices the water drips, but rather than rush to stop the dripping or move from his spot, he slowly takes his hat off and lets the accumulated water run across the brim of his hat and into his mouth because the day is too hot and he needs the moisture. The third man (Al Mulock) runs his hands slowly through the water trough. It is clear to us that the men are “bad guys.” We know this from their intimidating actions and their control of a space that does not belong to them. They are clearly waiting for the arrival of a train. The windmill in the background squeaks awkwardly. It is a sound effect edited in to have an eerie rhythm that does not match the action but is hypnotic. The footsteps are deliberate and heavy. The plank road is creaky and vacant. The scene is heavy with Foley. The sounds tell the story and support the visual compositions and characters. When the train arrives, the men see bags being taken off the train and travelers disembarking, but whoever they are waiting for does not appear. Finally, after the train begins to roll away, we notice our protagonist (Charles Bronson) because we hear him playing his harmonica. The three men turn slowly to look at him, and some dialogue ensues. Elam’s character taunts Bronson’s character, “Looks like we’re shy one horse.” Bronson replies after slowly shaking his head, “You brought one too many.” The guns are shot, bodies fall down, and the story begins. It is a bold beginning to a film.

Leone replaced all of the sound in all of his films. He preferred to affect the sonic style and have an intentionally artificial sense, yet the sound always progresses the action. The Foley and the edited sound effects are essential characters in all of his films, and this unusual opening illustrates his artful technique. According to Italian Foley artist Marco Ciorba, his father Aldo was one of the Foley artists who worked on Leone’s films. The practice on Leone’s films, reportedly, was to allow the Foley artist to visit the set and watch the actors perform to get a better sense of how they moved and on what surfaces they walked.

Another film that includes a classic example of Foley driving the story is All That Jazz (1979). In a key scene, we see the main character, a choreographer-director (modeled after the film’s director, Bob Fosse), in a table reading of his latest play. Joe Gideon (Roy Scheider) sits at the table, and for the next couple of minutes all we hear is the subjective sounds from his personal perspective, which relay sonic information essential to the storyline. The dialogue and actions of the other characters are a part of the scene but are not heard. Gideon is beginning the table read of his next production, and while we see his cast mouthing the lines from the play and notice their enthusiastic reactions to the script, we are drawn into Gideon’s personal physical experience. His chair creaks. He taps his hand on the table. We hear the amplified ticking of his watch, as he is clearly physically uncomfortable and not paying attention to the actors. He walks to the stairway and grabs the metal rail, then digs his fingers into the rail. He sits down again. He crushes his cigarette into an ashtray and lights a new one with a match. He then grabs his wrist. He drops his second cigarette to the floor and crushes it with his foot. He breaks his pencil into two pieces and drops it on the floor. He breathes heavily. He sweats. We see the concerned look on his ex-wife’s face as she is participating in the play reading. Then, the sound becomes objective again, including dialogue and sound from the room as the actors finish the reading and get up to walk away, chatting happily. We have just witnessed the character having a heart attack, yet not one word is spoken. With the exception of a few edited sound effects, it is all said with the camera and Foley, as performed by sound professional Elisha Birnbaum.

“It is essential to understand how the genre and style affect the choices and execution.”

The genre of comical farce utilizes Foley to emphasize character, humor, and plot in Peter Bogdonavich’s Noises Off (1992). The film begins as we watch actors rehearsing a farce that is going on the road to prepare for its debut in New York. The characters are introduced to us as they rehearse the first act of the play being performed, and then we witness the inner dramas between the performers, which add to the farce of the film, as they perform for various audiences. The characters, their relationships, and their idiosyncrasies are set up in the first 20 minutes. During the rest of the film, the relationships deteriorate. The farce is now backstage as well as onstage. The disintegration of the characters and their shenanigans are summarily revealed to us while they are backstage and cannot speak during a performance of the play in Florida. They race up and down the set stairs, pick up and set down plates of sardines, handle newspapers, fiddle with flowers, take slugs from a whiskey bottle, tear off clothes, put clothes on, slap each other, tie shoelaces, grab an axe, fall down the stairs, search for a contact lens, and fall over a sofa. The play is still being performed onstage while the movie audience watches the offstage activity. The sounds the movie audience hears are almost entirely Foley. The comedic use of the props and the exaggerated expressions of the actors backstage are heightened by the Foley as it supports the comedy with hyperbole.

The examples from these three films are different in genres. One is an epic western, one is a dramatic musical, and the third is a comedic romp. They use Foley in very different styles. The western is using Foley in a stylized manner, the musical in a realistic but heightened manner, and the comedy in a slapstick manner. Yet all three films have a distinct vision for the Foley to follow that supports the script, direction, and acting. The Foley serves the vision of the director and is the supporting player to the actors, the picture editor, and the sound editor.

When one is designing the sound effects, backgrounds, and Foley for a project, it is essential to understand how the genre and style affect the choices and execution. While the use of a sound effects library is essential for edited effects, Foley is intended to be a sonic performance, with all the life and character the actor has embodied in the scene. Rather than relying on picking sounds from a library, editing them in, performing the pedestrian Foley footsteps and hand props, and adding music to progress the scenes, much more is involved in the symphony of sounds that completes the film’s soundscape. The writer, director, production sound mixer, director of photography, and picture editor have all had an influence on shaping the drama, which the postproduction sound team must now support with sound effects, special sound designs, Foley, automated dialogue replacement (ADR), and a pristinely edited dialogue track. The soundtrack supports, drives, and influences the story, and in retrospect, we can analyze how Foley, as well as edited effects, was used to sonically portray the dramatic purpose of various films.

In Dead Calm (1989), we see how the suspense is reliant on sound. In one scene, we watch our lead protagonist intensely use drafting tools to analyze the sketch of the boat he is on. While a video is playing in the background (which features the psychopathic murderer in the film), our character walks through the boat, now filling with water, and proceeds to the bottom, which is almost submerged. He is swimming in the water and jams his knife through the boat bottom.

Later, our protagonist is spilling gasoline into the ocean and sets the abandoned boat on fire by throwing a lighted lamp onto the ocean, so as to attract the attention of his wife who is sailing toward him. The psychopath, meanwhile, is throwing the entire force of his body against the door of the room in which he is locked and scrapes the ropes off his hands. He crashes through the door, while wooden debris flies everywhere.

The sonic story adds to the suspense because there is virtually no dialogue and an understated musical score. The director relies on sound to help us enter the psychological space of the characters. The Foley is clean, specific, and realistic. Every edited effect is precise. The style is natural yet foreboding. Any overplayed sound effect, whether edited or Foleyed, would have diminished the tension of the scenes.

In John Carpenter’s The Thing (1982), we encounter a dog that has been absorbed by the “creature.” We watch a dog, accompanied by a crewmate, walk cautiously into a caged area with other dogs. The human footsteps are distinct and precise. This “good dog” is apprehensive as he enters the space. His footsteps are clear and prominent. Another dog, already in the room, begins to growl and threaten, his body writhing and transforming. We hear the “gush, gunk, and gore” that have nowadays become predictable. Accompanying the gore are cicada sounds that are edited effects. The good dog tries to free himself by chewing his way through the metal mesh enclosure, which is a Foley effect. The result is a carefully orchestrated series of sound effects and Foley that together form an auditory “skin crawling” so necessary to the story.

In Poltergeist (1982), we watch as many toys in a child’s bedroom fly through the air and slam into a closet. The music score is loud and obtrusive, so the prop sounds for the toys are equally loud and over-played. At the end of the scene, the characters handle a simple clown doll as they search for their daughter, who has disappeared. As the action dies down and the worst of the noise and turmoil is over, the irony of the clown being a loved and harmless toy is illustrated by the childlike and playful jingle added to his hat. The Foley performed for the scene adds to the chaos, then calm, of the psychic phenomenon. Underplayed and realistic Foley would have fallen short of the required task.

Musical Sound

Old-fashioned film musicals utilized a lot of Foley for the dance sequences. In Singin’ in the Rain (1952), Donald O’Connor’s performance in “Make ‘em Laugh” is a combination of production sound, “looping” of some dialogue, dance Foley, and prop work. The beginning of the scene has O’Connor and Gene Kelly walking in front of set pieces while talking. This dialogue leads into O’Connor’s song, where he sits on a piano, sings, fiddles with various items, and dances. All of his hand props and his dancing are Foley. The piano keys he steps on and his singing are from a prerecorded track. What is notable is that the background props are not audible. The Foley focuses on the foreground props. The lack of background props and footsteps is striking by today’s cinematic tastes. There is an artificiality to the track that was totally acceptable to the taste and genre of the 1950s.

White Nights (1985), on the other hand, has a scene that demonstrates a similar purpose for Foley footsteps and dancing, but for a different genre. This film is a musical drama. Gregory Hines plays an expatriate American living in the Soviet Union in the 1980s. He tells his bitter story of how he left the United States with dance. He narrates his story by dramatizing his childhood, experiences in Vietnam, and devolvement into a desperate life. The production track has Hines’s footsteps and scuffs in the track. He enhanced his dancing on a custom-made stage, and I Foleyed his footsteps for the scene. The final soundtrack consists of a blend of production footsteps, Foley footsteps, and Foleyed dance steps. The purpose of the dance is unique, and the hybrid of the three layers of footsteps is unusual. The goal was to sound natural and subtle.

“The use of direct sound for the vocals does indeed alter what is required from postproduction sound.”

Although both films combined production, dance Foley, and traditional Foley footsteps and props, the styles are very different. Both films succeed in their sonic goals because the craftspeople involved understood the purpose and style of the films and knew how to utilize the technology of the times appropriately.

When Les Misérables was released in 2012, there was great anticipation about the effect of such an operatic musical being produced with direct sound, reminiscent of what Rouben Mamoulian had attempted with the 1929 film Applause. Rather than utilizing prerecorded vocals, Tom Hooper shot the film with all vocals sung more intimately on the set. The use of direct sound for the vocals does indeed alter what is required from postproduction sound. When the singing is saved as dialogue rather than layered as a separate prerecorded track, it complicates how Foley and sound effects need to be designed to sound as natural and “earthy” as the singing itself does.

Episodic Sound

House of Cards, Netflix’s 2013 entry into episodic series, has changed the nature of “television” both for the consumer and for the producer. For those in postproduction sound, the workflow of the sound design has changed dramatically. Rather than the production of one episode at a time, for the purpose of the normal practice of weekly exhibition, the entire series became available at once. Several crews work on the postproduction sound simultaneously. Thus, the sound design, while possibly overseen by one sound supervisor, is less likely to be consistent. With such a task, it is easy to see how various Foley artists can be working on the same series at the same time and have different perspectives on how the characters should be sonically portrayed. How this immediate sound challenge will be worked out in the future is unclear, but for now, it presents questions regarding quality control. The traditional model, that of one sound supervisor with a consistent sound editorial crew and Foley artists who create a sonic story world—much like a composer creates the musical themes for the show—creates more artistic stability.

Cultural Sound

Styles in sound reflect styles in cultures. As was illustrated in Leone’s Italian-produced films, most of the sound and dialogue were replaced. This has been a frequent experience in Italian cinema. The sound has not been a primary concern in production; rather, it would be addressed mostly in postproduction. Some French films, on the other hand, seem to have an impressionism style that is utilized in the approach to sound. In Au Revoir Les Enfants (1987), Foley is added in an indicative manner. When the young boys in the school are walking around atop walking stilts, there is a general sense of the prop, but one cannot really discern one character’s actions from another’s. When they are eating in the dining area, there is a sense of activity with the bowls of food and eating, yet there is no clear accounting of who is doing what.

In Run Lola Run (1998), the fast cross-cutting requires a different task of the sound design and Foley. The sounds are specific and precise. Each detail of Lola’s three “runs” is essential to the viewer’s understanding of the plot. The Foley adds distinction to Lola’s escapades, which allows clearer comprehension as the narrative evolves to the climax and conclusion.

Dancer in the Dark (2000), and other films by Lars von Trier, offers some interesting situations regarding Foley utilization. As one of the co-creators of Dogme 95, with its manifesto regarding naturalistic film-making, von Trier’s rules were disregarded in Dancer in the Dark when he included singing, artificial sound design, and Foley. Julien Naudin has been von Trier’s preferred Foley artist.

As globalization spreads in the film world, international film communities have the opportunity to influence and be influenced by each other. However, there are distinctions because the cultures have different values. These values are expressed in the scripts, the cinematography, the acting style, the lighting, the costumes, the music, and the sound. While we have become more homogenized technologically, to assume that this equates to a homogenization sonically would be a mistake. Sound design presents a different style and perspective relative to geography, history, culture, economy, and politics. Foley artists all over the world have similarities in their goals, but their approaches vary greatly depending on their location, film market, and economic imperatives.

SINGLE OR MARRIED FOLEY AND SOUND EFFECTS

When most professionals started utilizing Foley in their soundtracks, what they usually had in mind was replacing or enhancing the footsteps of the characters and performing a few hand props. This was the original role of Foley. It is essential to understand when Foley is relied upon to enhance or “sweeten” the production track and when it is an essential part of the layering of sound effects. Additionally, Foley is becoming relied upon as a viable option for special sound design. As is true with any art form or craft, it evolves and transforms into something more than its original purpose. Foley can stand on its own in a track and assist in creating the reality of the story world, it can be layered atop the production track to enhance the story world, and it can be combined with the edited sound effects to create hyperreality.

Supporting Reality

When Foley is performed to replace or enhance the footsteps, one can assume that it is going to be played with the production track. Thus, the footsteps must be performed with the intent of “fitting in” with the dialogue as it is already recorded. This entails watching the sync very carefully and playing the recorded Foley back with production on the stage to ensure that it does indeed enhance and not detract.

“Consider the story. The story dictates everything.”

This is also the case for most hand props that are performed. If the artist is handling a gun or flipping pages in a book, for example, the performance should be realistic and subtle. The Foley artist has to duplicate—as closely as possible—whatever the actor is handling. A heavy cup in the film is a heavy cup on the Foley stage. If there is fine china in the scene, the dishes should sound fine as well. Watch the actor’s energy and movement and consider the story. The story dictates everything. When the Foley artist is recreating a prop that is in the scene already, then the goal is for the Foley to be indistinguishable from what is already in the production track.

Foley, however, has been increasingly used to complement edited sound effects. A car crash, for instance, might be created partially from the sound captured in production, partially from edited effects from a sound effects library, and partially from some field recordings that were ordered specifically for that scene. The Foley might then be asked to add the personality and life of that particular car crash. It is even possible that tireless and talented Foley artists would create that crash entirely on their own—given the time and access to the ideal props—for an assortment of reasons. The lesson here? There are no rules. There are conventions that are used, but they do not dictate the only possible approach.

When a Foley effect is to be played without an accompanying sound effect, either with the production track or even in a scene shot MOS (mit out sound), professionals refer to it as a single effect. However, it becomes a married effect if it is going to be layered with another prop or predubbed with another track of Foley or edited sound effects. This distinction becomes crucial for the Foley artist and mixer. Is the Foley being relied on by itself? Is this a domestic track or “foreign”? Will there be ADR? Keep these distinctions clear. Which part of the sound team is responsible for which part of the sound?

A married Foley effect might also occur when two sounds will be performed at the same time on one track. One artist might be manipulating the hinge squeaks used in a swing while the other works the chains. One might work the chairs creaking in a classroom while another scrapes the legs of the chairs against the floor. When the Foley is supporting reality, less is more. The Foley is not the star.

Enhancing Reality

Sometimes a supervisor has it in mind to have the Foley cover what is traditionally edited from library effects or customized field recordings. In Sylvester (1985), a film about a girl and her horse, some of the scenes had excellent production horse footsteps. The supervisor was not certain how his library horse footstep effects would work so, as an alternative, I Foleyed all of the horse footsteps. Now this has been done before, but sometimes editors would edit horses in from various library effects for time and cost-efficiency. The supervisor had decided to have additional “coverage” from several sources so the decision could be made during the final dub. What resulted was that some scenes were production sound, some were library effects, some were Foley (single track, single effect), and some were Foley married to the sound effects. The Foley artist should do whatever works best. To enhance reality means working with the reality of the sound but adding to it to give it an extra bit of character so it works better with the story.

A typical effect that is a married effect between Foley and sound effects is the ever-present body fall. Typically, the Foley artist will be down on the ground with some cloth or padding and will use the weight of the trunk of their body to thrust with control onto the floor. This performance will be combined with whatever has been cut in effects, which is usually some heavy thump sound of one or two tracks. The result is a body fall with the character from Foley and the weight from sound effects. Body falls never sound like they would in real life. This is where it is essential to enhance reality. When a body fall is in a film, it is for the purpose of creating dramatic effect. The goal is to create a body fall that is hyperreal. It sounds bigger and louder than a real body fall, but not so illogical that it takes the viewer out of the story. Notice that with sound the importance of the story is continually stressed. Sound supports the narrative. Always.

In Die Hard (1988), there is a scene in which Al, the friendly Los Angeles police officer, has a dead body land on his car. He frantically tries to drive away and ends up crashing his car. Supervising sound editor Steve Flick had his editors cut in most of the crash, but we sweetened it with some Foley stage hits against the car, the crunching of the metal and squeaking of the car door. Once again, this was a married effect. However, this scene was full of action combined with humor to reflect what was included in the script.

Replacing Reality

When a film or show requires a newly imagined aural reality, the Foley needs to reflect that reality with the imagined characters. This happens most often when the character is not a real person, but is a monster, scifi character, vampire, or some other creature that needs an imaginary sonic life. Foley is an imperative at this point. When Foley takes on this responsibility, the artist needs to thoroughly understand the narrative in total. What happens to this character? Where is the character, and how do the others relate to this character? Nothing about this character is simple. This is a challenge and can be great fun.

In Predator (1987), the mysterious hunter’s feet had to be performed first in Foley and then married to a heavy impact edited from sound effects. The result was a very heavy sounding footfall with more impact than we can artfully get on the stage, but with a naturalness and litheness that matched the character. It should be noted that Kevin Peter Hall portrayed the predator wearing a leotard in the black-and-white duplicate film print. It was only as the sound crew got further into the project that more of the visual design of the monster was evident. This is a critical point. Foley artists may not know the exact movements of the character when these situations arise. Sound professionals may be creating their sound design while watching someone dressed in an odd leotard or such and connected to wires and cables. They may not always see the final rendering. Once again, it is important to find out what the character will ultimately be. It might be someone like Andy Serkis working with motion capture.

Dave Stone, who co-supervised Predator with Richard Anderson, recollects another illustration of a married effect for replacing reality:

John P. [Pospisil] called the funny gun on the Predator’s shoulder a “Parrot Gun” because it looked like a pirate’s parrot. He had to create a special set of design effects for it. It had to sound like it was moved by “high-tech magic.” So, on the far away shots, we used just John P.’s work. But in the close-ups, we combined it with Foley because it needed more specific actions. My inclination is to use Foley to anchor things like that in reality. Foley adds character, personality and texture to cut sound effects that can be lifeless without it. But how intentional that is depends on the unique moment in the reel.

“They create and edit with their bodies.”

If an artist is performing Foley for a domestic release of a film or television show, then the task of Foley is usually simpler. It is assumed there will be less time for replacing sound, so as much of the production sound as possible will be used. Thus, Foley is responsible for less. However, if the language in the show is going to be replaced, then any sound that cannot be separated from dialogue must also be replaced. In today’s global marketplace, it is safe to assume that a foreign language may be replacing the original language, and a complete “music and effects” sound design task is required.

At one time or another, Foley artists have been asked to perform their Foley with such precision that they are like sound editors without Pro Tools. They create and edit with their bodies. The sounds they make are sometimes expected to stand on their own. But often, their performed effects will be married to edited sound effects that they have never even heard. Flexibility is a useful quality in a Foley artist. The artist might need to focus on sync or create an imaginary sound with no regard for sync whatsoever.

Foley can also be recorded “wild.” This entails recording a series of various performances of a prop to be edited later as a library effect. In actuality, most of the sound effects that end up in the sound library were at one time either field recordings or Foleyed effects. Glass clinks, gun clicks, background dishes, background cop gear, papers rustling, desks creaking, and other such sounds are background sound effects that were, at one time, performed as wild Foley on a stage by a Foley artist or sound editor. Alan Splet, the late and very great sound designer, was forever shooting new sound effects in the field or on a Foley stage for each of his films. To record and edit fresh sounds for his projects was one of his signature goals for a film. These recordings are now all in his comprehensive sound effects library, which is faithfully tended by his widow and fellow sound maven Ann Kroeber.

Creating Reality

On some of the more expensive and epic films, Foley is often relied on to create many of the effects typically layered and edited in. The reasons for this practice vary. Sometimes the effects are quite special and specific and relate to a character. In Explorers (1985), the spider’s footsteps had to be unique. Tim Sadler, the Foley mixer, had been a music engineer and had creative ideas that derived from that past career. John Roesch and Tim came up with a plan to have a very musical sound for the footsteps. David Stone remembers, “it sounded like fiberglass tubing and that John had manipulated it so it gave off different musical sounds. Each footstep had a different pitch.” At first, Stone and supervisor Mark Mangini figured they would marry the Foley with a designed sound effect by Pospisil. But as they recollect now, they think they relied totally on the Foley because it was so inventive.

Charles L. (Chuck) Campbell was known for requesting special sounds from the stage. Roesch had created many remarkable customized effects for Campbell using the Foley stage as a laboratory. Campbell would be at every Foley session and pursued his contribution as a Foley “director.” Not only did Campbell have his Foley artists audition shoes for each character but he also gave a lot of input on how he wanted the sounds designed.

It is practical and efficient to utilize a sound effects library for the more typical sounds found in a scene. A creative and dedicated editor can create magic with some imagination and well-tuned Pro Tools for the majority of the requisite sounds in most film and television projects. Yet, there are those who prefer to use the Foley stage for sound design, and this practice has become more common. In the 1980s and into the mid-1990s, time and money were spent on the Foley stage without much complaint. As schedules have tightened and money is scarcer, fewer films allow for the creative indulgences of editors or artists. Fortunately, many of these innovative effects have been preserved in libraries to be used again.

Yet, this practice is finding a resurgence in the Foley world. Foley artists are asked to create more and more of the effects on the stage. As editors have limited time for their tasks, they ask more of the Foley artists. The upside is that the Foley effects are fresh and personal to the film. Field recordist and editor Charles Maynes cites sound designers such as Ren Klyce as continuing the practice of having a lot of sound designed on the Foley stage. Maynes remarks, “Ren really takes advantage of what Foley can bring to the table.” Maynes adds that Foley is “largely used to make up for what production does not provide.” The downside is that Foley stage time is expensive and it takes a great deal of time to create sound for a film. Another possible issue with this approach is that there might be sound editors back in the cutting rooms working to make magic with the library effects themselves. More than once, ambitious Foley artists have created something wonderful on the stage, only to have it discarded at the dub stage because the sound effects had already been predubbed and the effect was effectively covered. The lesson here? Communication. Again, who is responsible for what in the soundtrack? Remember, there are conventions, but no hard and fast rules.

PART | TWO

How Foley Artists Work

Protocols, Conventions, and Hierarchy of Foley

Chapter | four

Fire in the Hole

The Players and Their Responsibilities

DOI: 10.4324/9781003008439-7

Postproduction sound works well when it has a systematic set of procedures. In Hollywood, there is a codified system that has been developed over many years. This system has variations depending on the budget and time allowed for the project. In general, however, most professional films and television shows in Hollywood follow the same paradigm. There is the accepted hierarchy of players and the expected conventions of the process. It is important to understand this system that lays out a common-sense approach for anyone who wants a tried-and-true blueprint for putting a soundtrack together. By understanding how the major players arrange their postproduction teams, an individual can more appropriately establish her or his best practices.

THE TEAM LEADER AND THE TEAM

Most films have a supervising sound editor (SSE). The production company, at the request of the director or picture editor, usually hires this person. Sometimes, in animation and television, the postproduction supervisor for the production company does the hiring. The important point is this: the first person hired for postproduction sound will be the supervisor. Some films have a sound designer and a supervising sound editor. The two positions have different purposes. The sound designer is responsible for very specific conceptual sound effects, and the supervising sound editor is the team leader for the rest of the sound crew. The SSE then hires the necessary editors for the show. The film may have a budget that allows for a generous supply of editors. More likely than not, however, the budget will be slim and a few, well-chosen editors will be hired. There will be someone to edit dialogue, someone to edit sound effects and backgrounds, someone to supervise and edit the automated dialogue replacement (ADR), and someone to edit the Foley. The music is composed and edited in a separate place by a separate crew. A music editor works with the film composer to select the proper cues for each musical motif. The composer and music editor do not normally collaborate with the sound team in Hollywood, but there are exceptions. In other film communities, such as New York or the San Francisco Bay Area, the composer and music editor might, indeed, collaborate regularly with the sound team.

On a big-budget show, the SSE will have the luxury of hiring more than one editor for each task. That is desirable as the schedules become shorter, more demanding, and more labor intensive. The crew may have to constantly change the edits of the film for “temp dubs” and picture changes.

The Foley artists are usually hired by the SSE. Normally, there are two Foley artists on a film or television show. However, if the budget is tight, it is not uncommon for one artist to have to do the entire show alone. This is not the preferred protocol, but budgets vary greatly, and some shows cannot afford the cost of two artists. In New York, there is typically one Foley artist.

The Foley artists are accountable to the supervising Foley editor. There may be another Foley editor, but the supervisor is in charge. This editor will have spotted the required Foley with the SSE and will have cued the Foley for the stage. Once the Foley has been performed and recorded, it is this editor who ensures that the Foley is edited and that all the cues are completed.

The Process of Each Team Player

The SSE spots the show with the director and picture editor. At this session, the SSE takes detailed notes on what is requested from the sound team. Notes are made regarding style, important dramatic moments, the specific conceptual ideas of the director, and continuity issues. The SSE may have the ADR editor at the session take notes on what lines the actors will need to replace during the ADR session. If the ADR editor is not at this spotting session, the SSE will have a separate spotting session later. The supervising Foley editor will also have a spotting session with the SSE.

The dialogue editors will get the most recent version of the film, television episode, or web series with the selected sound “takes” from the picture editor. In the days of analog sound, they would then divide up the reels to be finessed and work carefully to smooth and separate out the dialogue in the film. With the transition to digital sound, the process is the same, but the execution is different. The sound can be separated nondestructively on tracks and played back together. Dialogue editing can save the other editors on the sound crew a lot of unnecessary work. By arranging the dialogue properly, the dialogue editor and the rerecording mixer can retain the footsteps and props that were recorded in production. Thus, the sound effects editors and Foley editors can put their efforts into the truly necessary effects that need to be added to the tracks.

The Foley editor will cue the footsteps, hand props, cloth and special props, and custom effects that need to be performed on the Foley stage. Specific tracks will be assigned for each character walked and the specific types of props performed. The cloth will be put in the last track assigned. Traditionally, cue sheets would be sent to the Foley stage that would lay out the blueprint for the Foley mixer and Foley artists. Previously, when Foley was recorded onto a 24-track tape, the cue sheets were handwritten. Nowadays, they are cued directly into the digital session (the standard in most major film markets is Pro Tools) and sent to the Foley stage. Sometimes, cue sheets can be printed out from these sessions for the Foley artists to preview and plan what shoes and props to use.

“The sound editors are also doing a bit of ‘mixing-in-the-box’.”

The Foley mixer (or recordist, if there is one on the stage) will then load the session into the computer from a drive or download it from a server. Some studios (and some film schools) have a server that uploads and downloads, so no external hard drives or flash drives are ever sent to the stage. Sometimes, the Foley artists prefer to have the Foley mixer “call out” the cues to them. If this is their practice, they will not bother with printing out the sheets.

While the Foley artists are performing cues on the stage, the sound effects editors are cutting in their effects. Traditionally, the SSE usually would have auditioned and assigned effects for the film, and the editors would be given their “kit” of effects in the form of a library. However, now editors can download sound effects and, if time is really tight, decide for themselves which effects they should use. It is their job to organize and layer the effects and create the soundtrack for each session. More often than not, if time is tight, the sound editors might also include “mixing-in-the-box” because the technology allows them to. Pro Tools is designed to encourage boundary crossing and the work-flow often relies on this fluidity.

Meanwhile, the ADR editor is on the stage getting the required replacement lines from the actors in the film and then going back to the cutting room and cutting in the selected takes. This, however, can also be done at the stage because the ADR mixer has Pro Tools and can “move” the line right there, if there is limited time and it is an easy fix. Also, if the ADR is long distance—as is often the case for television shows and films shot in cities such as Atlanta—the ADR mixer, whose job is executed similarly to that of the Foley mixer, can record the ADR and send it online to the studio in Los Angeles edited and ready to go by the next morning. Once again, technology is allowing some alterations in the workflow.

The point is that all of the sound editors, whether they are cutting dialogue, effects, ADR, or Foley, are doing their tasks collaboratively but also simultaneously. They have to coordinate their sessions and must know what each other is doing. The best way to understand this unique form of collaboration is that the team works in unison, but not necessarily together.

Team Collaboration

All of the members of the sound team have to have a working knowledge of what the other members are responsible for so that there is no overlap in work effort, but everything is covered. The sound effects editors need to know what the dialogue editors are “saving” in production. The Foley editors need to know what the sound editors are cutting in from effects. The sound effects and Foley editors need to know where the music will be loud. All of the editors need to know what lines are being replaced. Everybody’s job affects everybody else.

“Everybody’s job affects everybody else.”

Once all the editors have edited their tracks, the tracks are sent to the dubbing stage to be predubbed and prepared for the final mix with the rerecording mixers. One mixer predubs the dialogue. That task can take a week or more. Another mixer predubs the effects and Foley. Then the ADR is predubbed. For the final mix, all rerecording mixers work together. One focuses on dialogue and ADR, and the other does the Foley and sound effects. One of them also mixes the music. On smaller or lower budget films, or in some film markets, one mixer does all of the mixing tasks.

Every editor has to be aware of how their material will dub. The editor is responsible to the mixer. Ideally, the SSE will sit on the dub stage during predubs to oversee the process and make sure the mixers have the material they need. The SSE might fix some edits on the mixing stage. The particular editor that worked on the session being mixed might make the changes. If the edits are minor, the mixers themselves may make the editorial adjustments.

“Anticipate the mix when presenting the Foley.”

The Foley artists and the Foley mixer should also be keenly aware of how the Foley will be dubbed at the final mix. Not only do the Foley artists and Foley mixer have to pay attention to the specific cues, but it is also essential that they know enough about the tasks of the editors and the mixers to be helpful. All too often, the Foley session arrives at the dub stage with too many tracks of Foley. The Foley is one aspect of sound. Do the Foley that is needed. No more, no less.

The Foley team needs to remember that the mixers at the final dub have the director, producer, picture editor, and others watching and contributing ideas during the dub. It is a stressful situation, and the best favor any Foley artist, Foley mixer, or Foley editor can do is to anticipate the final mix when presenting the Foley. A consideration of how much material should be included and, more importantly, not included aids the rerecording mixer a great deal.

In television or episodic web series, the situation is similar, except that everything is happening at once and on a compressed schedule. It is not uncommon for a television show to “turn over” one week before it is supposed to air. When a web series is produced with an intended single release date, the workflow becomes even more crucial and includes more variables regarding who will be working on what aspect of the sound. It is not uncommon for a series to have more than one sound team and one rerecording stage working simultaneously. Under these circumstances, consistency is difficult, at best.

Thus, it is even more essential that the postproduction sound team be organized and aware of each other’s tasks. It is also helpful to have little, if any, duplication of an effect. It should be covered in the production track, the effects tracks, or the Foley tracks.

There are many stories of a dubbing mixer having three or four versions of a sound. Perhaps the sound effects editor covered it several ways, as did the Foley team. Options might be offered when the sound team is unclear what works best. The decision has to be made—on the dub stage, under the watchful eye of the producer paying for the stage time, and with intense time pressure—what effects to use. As Dave Stone relates, Mike Finnell, producer for Joe Dante, once said, “I have to pay someone to cue it, then pay someone to cut it, then pay someone to get rid of it.”

Then, of course, there is the famous, yet perhaps apocryphal, story of Richard Portman, a revered rerecording mixer, who simply threw out tracks 13–24 of the Foley and only dubbed tracks 1–12. It was neither the first nor the last time that a mixer has been burdened with the task of dealing with too many cues that might not have needed to be performed at all.

ALTERNATIVE WORKFLOWS

Now that you have read about how the traditional workflow is established, there are some variations in how postproduction sound is approached that may be useful to you as you prepare your projects. While the Hollywood system is the most established and consistent, it would be a mistake to think it is the only way you can manage a soundtrack.

For an independent film, the context is different, so the protocols are different. The person in charge of sound is often the same person who composed the music or wrote the script. Perhaps the mixer is also the editor. Also, it is typical on a small film that some of the team members are less experienced. Thus, although the film is smaller and the budget is miniscule, it is helpful for independent filmmakers to understand what the professionals in “the big show” do so they can adapt the practices to their own projects.

The most important step is to have one person in charge of organizing the soundtrack. That person acts as the supervisor. The supervisor can also edit, or perform the Foley, or mix, but there needs to be one person who is the team leader and point person.

WHAT EFFECTS NEED TO BE COVERED AND BY WHOM

Once the leader is established, the organization of the soundtrack can vary. The Foley artist can cue and edit the Foley as well. This is actually an excellent idea. By editing your own Foley, and even mixing it, you can learn more about what works appropriately for a project and what does not. The Foley mixer can mix the final dub. The editors can predub in Pro Tools. The dialogue editor can also compose the music. The configuration of skills can vary, but the tasks and accountability cannot. The system used in features and television is tried and tested. It works very well and is a good template for any project. The main difference is that there will be fewer people and they will multitask. Some of the biggest names in Hollywood learned filmmaking this way by working with Roger Corman. It is a great way to learn.

One of the first issues that will come up when spotting a project for sound is what effects need to covered and by whom. A very experienced dialogue editor knows how to “save” a lot of the production sound, and the effects and Foley editors will not need to cover as much material. More typically, however, there will be plenty of issues in the production sound that will require enhancements from the Foley artists.

“Dialogue editing is a fine art.”

Dialogue editing is a fine art, and if improperly executed it can yield disastrous results. Dialogue that is “overcleaned”—that is, does not have enough background noise—will sound sterile. A sterile background means sound effects, backgrounds, and Foley will have to take more responsibility. If the dialogue is muddy and sloppily edited, then sound effects, backgrounds, and Foley have to top the production and add even more layers of sound.

If the editors cutting sound effects do not communicate properly with the Foley team, then sounds will be made on the Foley stage that duplicate the sound effects that are already on the tracks. It is a common problem for the rerecording mixers to have to slow down the predub process to hunt for the effects. Are they covered in Foley? Production? Sound effects? Not at all?

The Foley stage is a wonderful laboratory to create unusual sounds, in addition to performing ordinary and uneventful sounds that create the sonic life of a character. It should also be essential to retain a sense of practicality. A Foley artist needs to be intuitive as well as analytical, but it is not always possible to be both at the same time. The key to being the best Foley artist is to balance the two. The important key is to remember that there will be people who handle the Foley after it is recorded. Editors and mixers will need the Foley well prepared and artfully executed.

Chapter | five

Let’s Go Ahead and Go Back

Spotting, Cueing, and Editing Foley

DOI: 10.4324/9781003008439-8

Foley artists begin their contribution to the sound design on the Foley stage. They collect their shoes and props for the session, go over any cue sheets, sip their coffees, perhaps devour a bagel, and begin to plan the first few cues. Normally, if the project is a live-action or animated film, there will be some time to view the beginning of the show to get a sense of the amount of work required to be completed before the session is finished.

If, however, the session is for a television show, a game, or an independent film that is directed by someone new, it is less predictable what the day—or evening—might entail. The reason for this is simple: features and long-form episodic shows typically have a planning stage, called a spotting session, and hopefully have some kind of codified cueing system. However, this is not as typical for a television show, a weekly cartoon show, a game, or an independent film. As technology allows for the workflow to speed up, nuances and niceties like spotting sessions, cue sheets, and clear communication between editors and Foley professionals can become expedited or even eliminated.

For this reason, there is a conceptual ideal situation for spotting and cuing Foley that can be utilized and can demonstrate why a spotting session and also specific cueing are so important for quality Foley. If the ideal does not occur, that is fine. Making do is par for the course in the entertainment arts. However, the more often standards are maintained, the more adjustments and accommodations can be made when necessary. The balance is to know what to modify and what to retain.

THE SPOTTING SESSION

The spotting session is where brainstorming meets ordinary details. Ideally, when a supervising sound editor (SSE) is hired to head a sound crew, they meet with the director and the picture editor. Sometimes, if time permits and the budget allows, the first picture assistant and ADR supervisor might attend the session as well. There are rare occasions when the Foley supervisor is at the first spotting session. However, the days of these kinds of spotting sessions are becoming rarer and rarer. We are now in a time when budget hawks do not want to pay for anything they consider extra or even trivial. Even though the spotting session can save thousands of dollars down the road for a typical major feature film, the idea of several sound professionals sitting around discussing sound ideas seems like an unnecessary expense to most accountants. It is not surprising that they would not understand the necessity of the preplanning that can save time and money at the back end of the final mix. Rather than being a frivolous time of playfully toying with sound design ideas, the opposite is more often the case. Most spotting sessions are much more serious and grueling than the budget hawks can even imagine.

“‘The sound nobody’s ever heard before’.”

The director and editor will usually run the picture and discuss preferences with the SSE. The SSE takes notes and asks about ADR that will be needed, special concerns, and timelines. There will be questions about temp dubs, reshoots, and picture changes. Often, the director will state the obvious to the sound editor about the pedestrian effects. But the key is that the SSE gets a clear sense of what the expectations are regarding sound.

David Stone relates a story about working with Tim Burton on Batman Returns (1992):

So, we were in Chris Lebenzon’s cutting room with Tim, and Rich Anderson and I were at the KEM table, and Chris and Tim were driving, going back and forth and Tim would say, “Oh, there’s a door. We have to get that door. Here’s a this and here’s a that.” And then he just stopped and turned to us and said, “Oh, what am I doing? You guys know what to do. Why don’t I just go to the places where I have concerns?” And Rich and I just turned to each other as though to say, “At last, a director with some common sense and some trust! He knows that we know how to cover the mundane things. That’s why he’s paying us.”

Stone continues,

Every movie has certain unusual concerns, what we used to call “the sound nobody’s ever heard before” because so many directors would make that point as though they were the first to ever say that. After—you’ve done a few movies, you begin to see these patterns. The supervisor can save the director a lot of time by acknowledging the special concerns and spend the time on the issues that really need to be discussed. We don’t get much time with the director, as a rule, and an empowering director, like Tim, will use our time together wisely.

During spotting sessions, Stone would write Foley notes for his Foley supervisor. He would then present the supervisor—on Batman Returns it was me—with a notebook containing a complete written breakdown of each reel, what the sound concerns were, what sound effects would cover, and what Foley would be responsible for performing. He scheduled a session with me to run the film and talk through concept, style, and schedule. I left the session knowing what was expected of Foley, who the effects editors were (in case I had to call about a specific cue), and how much I had to accomplish each day on the stage. This is the more financially high-end approach to spotting.

Stone held similar sessions for ADR. At these meetings, the ADR supervisor was told which lines would be replaced in ADR and which lines the dialogue editors would probably be able to “save” in the production tracks. All editors had a notebook. All editors knew who was on the crew, where they were, and what they were cutting in. It is a seamless approach that allows maximum empowerment for the editors because the communication is so complete.

“‘Let’s talk about the foliage’.”

Each SSE has their own approach for spotting with the crew. Stone says,

I [would] try to approach a piece of work in many passes. So, for instance, I might try to sit with the person in charge of the Foley cueing and first just discuss the “big deal” questions. I like to layer the work.

He continues, “For instance, ‘We’re going to do a jungle picture like Predator. Let’s talk about the foliage. Let’s make that a special treatment and get fresh foliage every day.’ Then I would go to another specific issue.”

Supervisor Don Sylvester, recipient of the Academy Award for Best Sound Effects Editing for his work on Ford v Ferrari (2019), discusses some of his various experiences with director James Mangold regarding sound:

Foley is entirely character driven. I always approach things wondering “what would Jim do?” How does it [sound] tell the story? Foley is one of Jim’s pet peeves because Foley is entirely character driven. It is character and he knows what the character would do, and if the Foley doesn’t know then he gets angry. In the previous movie Logan, there is a very strong girl, and he tries to take her backpack away from her, and they pull back and forth a few times and finally he rips it out of her hand. That was really difficult to do for us because as Jim explains it, there are two titans fighting and the backpack didn’t have a chance … that was our instruction … we were establishing character and that was a plot point … the backpack ripping and tearing and that isn’t happening on screen … the only way you could sell the way they fight is if you rip the backpack while they’re fighting.

According to Sylvester, director Mangold has a different kind of process for relating his preferences for sound in his films:

He’ll sometimes alert us to some things we miss … but now my goal is to find it first … so what he asks us to do is to take a pass at things, and then he will come in and critique it … he doesn’t tell us what he wants before. It works better that way because sometimes we get it right (laughs).

Sylvester comments on the car race sound effects in Ford v Ferrari:

What is the best way to tell the story? Sound effects is based on character and story. Do they qualify as an integral part of advancing the plot? For example, there is a time and place for loud car sounds. We are very aware of that. I didn’t want to break the rules of “this is not storytelling, this is just sound making.”

While supervising 3:10 to Yuma (2007), Sylvester relates that as Ben Wade (Russell Crowe) comes down the stairs, the Foley was performed so that “even the stairs were reacting to him [Crowe].”

Nowadays, this kind of detailed preparation and aesthetics discussion is rare. It takes time and experience that many do not have in today’s sped-up and squeezed budget realities. But the emphasis is on organization and communication. Maximum efficiency and creativity can be attained if the housekeeping aspects of sound are honored. While this kind of care cannot always be attained, recent historical context can remind us just how far we have gotten away from being mindful of what is really required from a first-rate postproduction soundtrack. Planning is crucial. Whatever the editor, artist, or mixer can do to get a plan down on paper—or on the computer screen—they are wise to do it.

FOCUSING THE TASKS

The same principles can be followed for smaller or independent films. The key is that there must be a leader. Ostensibly, there needs to be the SSE who functions as the go-to person for everything. An experienced first assistant is a necessity as well. Even if a project has only a few individuals editing an entire film, a strong SSE and first assistant will ensure that the show is executed efficiently.

Mark Pappas relates that spotting sessions are also being modified to accommodate tighter budgets:

Normally, we hope that the supervisor of a show is able to spot with the director, but that happens less and less nowadays. What happens is the picture editor is whom they may spot with. We get the instructions from the picture editor. Then after the first temp, you actually hear things that the director might say. If there is a second temp or [we] go right into pre-dubs, we hope we have it remedied by that point.

A really excellent soundtrack to study is Road to Perdition (2002). It is artful, elegant, and unobvious. Scott Hecker was the SSE. Spotting

sessions can reveal the director’s concept about the sonic narrative. This film is an example of sound design that is a result of intuitive communication between Hecker and the director, Sam Mendes. Hecker relates how Mendes described his sonic aspirations at their first spotting session: “When I was working with Sam initially, he said, ‘Scott, I want the sound to be poetic, not prosaic. I want it to be operatic, not episodic.’” When a director gives imagery like this, it is inspiring, to say the least. Hecker’s sound supervision on the film, earned him an Oscar nomination.

“He wanted each race to sound different.”

Another film Hecker supervised was Without Limits (1998), directed by Robert Towne. The film is about Olympic runner Steve Prefontaine, and there are several sequences where the film shows him running. Hecker assumed he would have the Foley (performed by his brother, Gary) cover all the footsteps of Prefontaine. Towne had another idea. He told Hecker he wanted the running to be done with sound design. Instead of a real-world runner with cleats and footsteps increasing with effort (the expected approach), he wanted each race to sound different.

Towne wanted the first race to sound like “fish feeding under water.” He wanted the second race to sound like “birds flapping wings on a plate of glass.” Hecker says, “I started out scared and walked out [of the spotting session] thinking, this is going to be cool! [Towne] wanted me to take a can opener to the top of my head and think outside the box.” While Hecker’s brother wanted to Foley all of the footsteps in a more traditional fashion, Hecker was firm and held to the concept Towne had expressed.

Greg Hedgepath, SSE for Poetic Justice (1993) and Selma (2014), has a general philosophy when preparing for a spotting session. He reads the script two times, making sound notes for the overall sound design, and asks himself, “what are the important elements?” He concerns himself with how sound weaves in and out of the scene. “It’s like making a painting,” he remarks. He may take some sounds out, then move them back in. “Is it going to be moving? Is it going to be static?” He considers background sounds to be very important and considers these sounds while making notes as well. For Hedgepath, Foley is best when “it doesn’t call attention to itself,” but is quick to add, “Foley adds life to the scene … it brings a scene back alive again.”

F. Hudson Miller, who says he “never wanted to be a sound guy, but I did want to be a film guy,” studied cinematography in film school and discovered he had an aptitude for sound. After working with veteran sound editors Sandy Berman and David Yewdall, he began editing Foley. Now a SSE, Miller states, “I don’t approach Foley and [sound] effects differently. The wonderful thing about Foley is it is performed to picture.” His approach to both cueing and editing Foley is the performance, although he admits he prefers less Foley in a film than some others might enjoy. “The most important thing is how much emotional content is in it … we tell stories for the heart and soul.” While working at Paramount, he fondly recalls his experiences with director Tony Scott, who he describes as “the most precise director I have ever worked with about what he wanted [from sound].”

Director Kevin Lima, who has a background directing animated films such as A Goofy Movie (1995), Tarzan (1999), and Enchanted (2007), comments, “you’re building a perception of a world … and aural landscape of our own choosing.” When considering the sound design for his movies, he is focused on “how to orchestrate those sounds depending on emotional context.” Like Miller, Lima believes the emotion should motivate sound in film. “Too much sound becomes distracting … sound and the character work hand in hand.” An example he gives is “how would this character grab their prop?”

It is unusual for a director to play a role in the Foley performance in Los Angeles, but not as unusual in New York. The Coen Brothers will collaborate with Foley artist Marko Costanzo frequently. Thinking back to the Foley for the movie Ishtar (1987), Costanzo recalls that director Elaine May was very “hands on” regarding Foley. “She knew what she wanted and would persist until she was satisfied.” In these situations, even if there is a bona fide spotting session, the actual cuing for the Foley is not executed by the Foley editor. Rather, the director sees the sonic performance as part of the actor’s character and includes the task in their role.

SPOTTING REDUX

Sometimes, a spotting session is for the intention of “repurposing” an already released film. When a film is going to be shown on network or cable television and has to be conformed to the commercial breaks or other time considerations, it sometimes has to be shortened. At other times, however, sound editors have to add footage that was not in the original release to expand the length of the film for television. This requires editing in sound effects, performing and editing Foley and ADR, and editing additional music cues.

The spotting sessions of repurposed films are short and sweet. The network provides simple notes, and we all conform to the new requirements. The trick is getting the new sound to match the old sound so it can then be remixed at the dub stage.

Sound editors get the stems from the original dub of the film and go back into the material to see what changes or additions were requested. Once they find sound effects that fit and match, Foley artists perform the necessary cues, and the editors edit them in. The same would be done for ADR. Another editor would be finding music cues that could be stretched or added to the new version.

There might also be a separate spotting session for the airline or television version of a film. This usually does not affect Foley much. The purpose is to replace objectionable language with new ADR lines that cover the old lines. If you have heard “Oh, that is freaking great” or “Just flake off,” you can be sure you were watching either the airline version or the repurposed television version. Sometimes you will even notice that words are missing. Now you know why.

CUEING FOR SHOEING

Once a Foley editor has the spotting notes, the next step is to translate these concepts and limitations into cue sheets to be used on the stage. Until recently, the more experienced and meticulous editors would present handwritten cue sheets to the Foley crew on the stage. The cue sheets were notated in vertical columns with the principal footstep cues beginning in tracks one, two, three, and so on. The middle tracks would be background footstep cues, and the outside tracks would be various props. The final track was a cloth pass.

Now, most cueing is done on a Pro Tools session. The tracks are horizontal, but the approach is the same. The first main character’s footsteps are on track one. The track assignments remain as they did previously. The main difference is that, instead of writing the cues in, the cues are marked into a session the session is saved on a hard drive or a flash drive, and sometimes it is sent to a server. The stage loads in the sessions from the server or the drive. The cues are then recorded right in the regions marked and tagged in Pro Tools. Cue sheets can be printed from the Pro Tools sessions.

It is not necessary to cue Foley to the first specific moment of movement. Foley artists will be looking at the action in the context of the scene. Whereas ADR is cued to the first “mod” of sound to better help the actor be in perfect sync, Foley can be cued more loosely. As long as one cues with the thought of helping the artist anticipate the action, the cueing will be appropriate. There is a danger to cueing too closely to the first footstep. Mixers and artists like a “breath” before the actual cue begins. Sometimes “streamers” are programmed into the recording equipment, which help the artist hit sync, but many Foley artists are used to “eyeballing” it. The important thing is to get close to the moment, not on top of it.

Another important trick to cueing is that it is better for the Foley artists if the cues have a flow to them. If the picture is edited to show a back-and-forth perspective (we see Jane walk down the hall, then Max in the kitchen, then Jane, then Max), it is easier for the Foley artists if they keep Jane’s cue going, rather than stopping it and cueing for the next cut. The walk will feel more natural and the artist will be more relaxed. Remember, Foley is a performance. Cueing for the artist and the mixer, not for the editor, is best practice.

Before the use of digital editing stations, when cues were handwritten, cueing was a slow, painstaking, monotonous, and, at times, boring process. However, it was also the most critical use of time in a Foley editor’s workflow. It would take about 4 hours for an experienced editor to cue 10 minutes of film. An animated film would take longer, as would an action sequence. Digital cueing is faster because the editor selects the recording track and types in the cue. The care that used to be taken to select the exact track is not as critical now because the Foley mixer can move tracks with ease on the stage and accommodate additional tracks to build a Foley effect. Also, schedules are shorter for Foley editors, and the result is that less time is used for cueing.

“Watch every scene several times.”

When editors cue Foley, they should watch every scene several times. All editors vary in their approach, but there are some conventions they keep in mind. First, they cue the main characters in the scene, character by character, keeping each character on a consistent track if possible.
